
===== [p58-59] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 5.3.1 Working principle (5块) =====
--[p58]-- 5.3.1 Working principle
A stationary placement location is used. The nozzle and the alignment system are attached to a sub‐frame or the machine base frame and are not actuated in the horizontal plane.
Using a beam splitter (e.g. semi –reflecting mirror), the image of both the die and substrate are captured on a single image processor<sup>11</sup> either simultaneously or subsequently.
--[p58]-- To make this concept work successfully, the optical length from camera to die and from camera to substrate should be equal. For this reason it is important to have an accurate height measurement of both the substrate relative to the beam splitter and the die to the beam splitter. This can be accomplished by moving the optics in vertical direction to focus cameras on substrate and additionally moving the nozzle to focus the die image.
--[p58]-- By superimposing the images of both locations, a measurement of their offset is obtained by looking at fiducials or TSV locations. The number of pixels between the markings is a measure of the XY offset of both locations. A second camera is used to look at another fiducial or TSV. This way the angular error can also be measured since obtaining this measurement from the XY camera is not possible due to the limited field of view.
--[p58]-- <sup>11</sup> The actual optical design is much more complicated. Using just a simple beam splitter would cause the image plane and the plane image sensor to differ, causing image distortions and limited focus depth. The actual optical design cannot be reported on since it is confidential.
Placement process:
1. Prepare
a. New die is attached to nozzle
b. Wafer stage presents next placement location
2. Relative measurement is taken from die to placement location with a camera system
a.
--[p59]-- One camera used for XY alignment
b. One camera used for rotational DOF
c. One camera to correct for height sensitivity of system
3. Final adjustment steps are made by wafer stage and nozzle
4. Die is placed with blind z‐stroke with high XYφ repeatability.
5. Nozzle is moved back up
Two drawbacks of this system are the large required z‐stroke of the φ‐z actuator and the blind placement stroke, since no measurement can be taken when the nozzle moves down.

===== [p42-43] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 4.1 Introducing parallelism (6块) =====
--[p42]-- 4.1 Introducing parallelism
The PnP cycle for flip chip contains the following actions, in the order performed by the Datacon machine:
A. Releasing die from source wafer
B. Picking die from source wafer
C. Transporting the die to the target location
D. Placing of the die (Bonding)
a. Alignment between die and stack
b. Place die
c. (Temporarily) bond die
 Flipping the die
Measurements to position die relative to target location
Four actions (A to D) have to be performed in chronological order.
--[p42]-- The transport of multiple dies to the placement location simultaneous is an example of performing a single action on multiple dies.
For a fully serial process, the cycle time is defined by:
$$
t _ { c y c l e \_ s e r i a l } = \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { m } \bigl ( a c t i o n _ { 1 } , a c t i o n _ { 2 } , . . . , a c t i o n _ { m } \bigr )
$$
The cycle time of a fully parallel process is equal to:
$$
--[p42]-- For a continuous process one can introduce even more parallelism by, for example, releasing one die while placing another one, provided these actions are performed by different systems. This reduces the total time needed to process a full wafer (increased throughput) while keeping the process time of a single die equal. Therefore, in the interest of accomplishing maximal throughput, there are three fundamental ways to reduce the effective cycle time.
Parallelize actions performed on a single die
--[p42]-- The other two, flip action and measurements, need to be performed before the actual placement, but can be placed between or parallel to any of the other steps. Therefore, when looking to actions performed on a single die, the first four actions have to be in series, while the other two can be either in series or parallel to another action.
--[p42]--  Handle multiple dies simultaneous
o Perform different actions on different dies simultaneous
o Perform the same action on multiple dies simultaneous
One example of a parallel action performed on a single die is for example the position measurement of a die while transporting it to the placement location. To perform different actions on different dies simultaneous, one can transport one die to the placement location while measuring the position and orientation of a different die.
--[p43]-- t _ { c y c l e \_ p a r a l l e l } = \operatorname* { m a x } \left( a c t i o n _ { 1 } , a c t i o n _ { 2 } , . . . , a c t i o n _ { m } \right)
$$
Clearly, the cycle time of a parallel process can be much less than that of a serial process, especially if the process steps have similar duration and if there are many process steps.

===== [p8-9] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > Samenvatting (18块) =====
--[p8]-- De assemblage (het stapelen) van de 3D‐IC is één van de processen met de grootste bijdrage aan de totale kosten. Om deze reden houdt een deel van het TNO 3D‐IC initiatief “BlueBird” zich bezig met het ontwikkelen van een ‘pick and place’ machine om deze assemblage te realiseren.
The ‘pick and place’ project bestaat uit twee delen. Allereerst, om de stapels van chips te laten werken, moeten de elektrische contacten in de vorm van ‘Through Silicon Vias’ (TSVs) goed contact maken.
--[p8]-- Samenvatting
Technologie wordt steeds belangrijker. Vooral de elektronica industrie heeft de afgelopen decennia, door de ontwikkeling van steeds kleinere en krachtigere producten, snel een belangrijke rol verkregen in ons dagelijks leven en zal dit voortzetten in de toekomst. Tegenwoordig bevatten de meeste elektronische apparaten ‘surface mount devices’. Met de hedendaagse miniaturisatie en toenemende prestaties is de verpakking van deze componenten de limiterende factor geworden.
--[p8]-- Om dit te realiseren zijn plaatsing nauwkeurigheden tot 1  μm nodig. Dit precisie onderwerp en bijbehorende metrologie is onderzocht in een parallel project. Het huidige project houdt zich bezig met het ontwikkelen van een nieuw assemblage concept dat de mogelijkheid heeft om vijf afzonderlijke chips per seconde te kunnen plaatsen bij gelijkblijvende machinekosten t.o.v. huidige machines. Deze toename in verwerkingscapaciteit is nodig om 3D‐IC economisch rendabel te maken.
--[p8]-- Door het parallel verwerken van meerdere dies en door het simultaan uitvoeren van verschillende acties op een enkele die was de verwachte doorlooptijd verkort en daarmee de verwerkingscapaciteit vergroot. Door de concepten onderling te vergelijken zijn drie potentiële concepten gekozen welke de meeste kans bieden de benodigde nauwkeurigheid en snelheid te halen. Deze concepten waren de volgende:
--[p9]-- Deze resultaten wijzen er op dat het mogelijk is het ontbrekende onderdeel van het nieuwe PnP concept te realiseren, de flexibele drager zelf.
De haalbare plaatsingsnauwkeurigheid en verwerkingscapaciteit van het nieuwe concept zijn ook onderzocht. Het is waarschijnlijk dat de beoogde specificaties van vijf dies per seconde en een plaatsingsnauwkeurigheid van 1  μm haalbaar zijn met het nieuwe concept. Dit maakt het economisch rendabel stapelen van dies tot 3D‐ICs mogelijk.
--[p9]-- Aangezien de Diels‐Alder lijm nog steeds in de onderzoeksfase is en daardoor de huidige eisen nog niet haalt is het elektrostatische concept verder onderzocht.
Om te onderzoeken of elektrostatische velden geschikt zijn om dies vast te houden, is een test opstelling ontwikkeld. De resultaten van de uitgevoerde experimenten laten goede overeenkomsten zien met de schattingen gebaseerd op theoretische wiskundige modellen.
--[p9]-- De mogelijkheid om het nieuwe concept met bestaande onderdelen te realiseren is onderzocht. Commercieel verkrijgbare onderdelen zijn voorgesteld voor de machine componenten welke beide hoge snelheid en nauwkeurigheid vereisten. Hierdoor is het aannemelijk gemaakt dat zowel de beoogde verwerkingscapaciteit als plaatsingsnauwkeurigheid gehaald kunnen worden. Het enige systeem waarvoor geen commercieel alternatief was, was het flexibele drager systeem.
--[p8]-- Om de huidige trends voort te zetten zijn innovatieve oplossingen nodig. Een veelbelovende techniek is ‘3‐Dimensional Integrated Circuit Technology’ (3D‐IC). Deze houdt zich bezig met het stapelen van dies (chips). Dit stapelen maakt de afstand tussen dies kleiner en vermindert daarmee de grootte van de verpakking terwijl de prestaties toenemen. Tegenwoordig bestaat er nog geen rendabele manier om 3D‐ICs te maken.
--[p8]-- Conclusies betreffende de werksnelheden zijn getrokken gebaseerd op de benodigde snelheden, acceleraties en jerk. Trillingsmetingen zijn uitgevoerd aan een commercieel verkrijgbare PnP machine om te bepalen of het huidige machine concept geschikt is voor de nieuwe eisen aan nauwkeurigheid en snelheid.
--[p9]-- Het wordt ook aanbevolen om het risico te onderzoeken dat de elektrische circuits op de die worden aangetast door het elektrische veld van de elektrostatische actuator. Dit is van groot belang aangezien het effect van een dergelijke actuator op een die met TSVs nog onbekend is.
--[p8]-- Door onderzoek naar huidige state‐of‐the‐art ‘pick and place’ (PnP) machines is duidelijk geworden dat commercieel verkrijgbare machines niet de juiste combinatie van snelheid en plaatsingsnauwkeurigheid hebben. Huidige machines voldoen echter vaak wel aan of het snelheid of het nauwkeurigheid criterium. Dit illustreert de wisselwerking tussen beide. Om de snelheidslimieten van huidige machine concepten te vinden zijn de bewegingsprofielen berekend.
--[p8]-- De uitkomst was dat een het huidige concept niet erg geschikt was om de gewenste nauwkeurigheid te halen, zeker niet wanneer de snelheden worden opgevoerd om de gewenste verwerkingscapaciteit te halen. Daarbij is nog een grote vooruitgang in actuator technologie nodig om de benodigde snelheden te halen.
Tien PnP concepten voor het plaatsen van dies op een wafer zijn ontwikkeld. Hierbij stond het gebruiken van parallelle acties centraal.
--[p9]-- Toekomstig onderzoek is nodig om het machine concept te realiseren. Verschillende onderwerpen zijn geïdentificeerd. Één hiervan is het aanpassen van het meetsysteem ontworpen voor 3 μm naar 1 μm. Een andere uitdaging is het werkelijke ontwerp van de flexibele drager en zijn aandrijving. Deze aandrijving is het onderwerp van een huidige afstudeer opdracht.
--[p8]-- Een draaiende turret tussen de oppak en de plaats positie zorgde voor een verkortte transport afstand en bood de mogelijkheid simultaan te pakken en plaatsen.
In het flexibele drager concept werd het transport van oppak naar plaats locatie voorzien door een tussenliggend systeem. Hiermee werd de timing en de werkelijke plaats van beide locaties los gekoppeld. Dit concept bood ook de mogelijkheid om extra processen uit te voeren tussen oppakken en plaatsen
--[p9]-- Eerst is onderzocht hoe een die met een tijdelijke verbinding vast te maken aan de drager. Hiervoor zijn verschillende mechanismen onderzocht, waarvan twee kansrijk waren. De eerste is een plakkende tape met herbruikbare, uitschakelbare lijm, gebaseerd op Diels‐Alder chemie. Het andere concept is een laag voltage elektrostatische actuator.
--[p9]-- Het flexibele drager concept had een voordeel in plaatsingsnauwkeurigheid (door lagere reactiekrachten) en in de mogelijkheid om parallelle processen uit te voeren. De werking van dit concept is als volgt:
De die wordt losgemaakt van de bron locatie en geplaatst op de flexibele drager. Deze drager transporteert de die naar de plaatslocatie terwijl additionele parallelle processen uitgevoerd kunnen worden (bijvoorbeeld schoonmaken en inspectie).
--[p9]-- Op de plaatsingslocatie wordt de die los gemaakt van de drager en met een phi‐z actuator geplaatst op de doel locatie, welke zich op een XY bewegingsplatform bevindt. De positiemeting van die en stapel wordt voorzien door een co‐locatie camera systeem, welke is ontwikkeld in het nauwkeurigheid deel van het BlueBird project. Dit enkele camera systeem heeft een zeer kleine meetlus. Hiermee wordt betere nauwkeurigheid verkregen dan met commercieel verkrijgbare systemen haalbaar is.
--[p9]-- De hoogste verwerkingscapaciteit werd gehaald door de transport afstand tot een minimum te beperken. In dit concept werden oppak en plaats locatie direct tegenover elkaar geplaatst en de componenten overbrugden de minimale afstand in minimale tijd. Nauwkeurigheid was een potentieel probleem met dit concept.
Na het verder bestuderen van deze drie concepten is het uiteindelijke concept gekozen door de voordelen en nadelen te vergelijken van de concepten.

===== [p55-56] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 5.1.2.2 Placing of die on target wafer (4块) =====
--[p55]-- 5.1.2.2 Placing of die on target wafer
To make things easy, the process of placing the die on a stack on the target wafer is very similar to the picking of the die. Two similar solutions are presented, in one of them the proposed mechanical stiffness of the flexible carrier is used. The other uses a common vacuum nozzle system.
1. Initial positioning
a. Flexible carrier is moved one die pitch
b. Wafer stage is moved one die pitch by an XY stage, to present next stack
2.
--[p56]-- Two possibilities are illustrated below (Fig. 5‐5) considering the case that the nozzle only makes a φz movement. One can either have a hole in the flexible carrier for the nozzle to fit through, or the die can be placed such that the backside is partially not covered by the carrier.
Which of both concepts is most useful is also dependent on the chosen clamping mechanism and how the actual alignment is performed. Therefore, no decision is made on this topic.
<sup>10</sup> For example; Wafer stage can be used if flexible carrier proves to have too high in plane stiffness
--[p56]-- High accuracy nozzle moves down and is attached to flexible carrier
3. Once the nozzle controls the position of the die, a relative measurement is performed between die or nozzle, and stack or wafer stage
4. Placement
a. Final XY adjustment is carried out by wafer stage or small XY nozzle motion
b. Final φ alignment performed by nozzle (preferred) or wafer stage (optional)<sup>10</sup>
c. Nozzle pushes die against stack with required bond force
5.
--[p56]-- Clamp force of flexible carrier is reduced and nozzle is moved up, leaving die attached to stack
Instead of the nozzle grasping the flexible carrier, it can attach to the die directly if the flexible carrier allows this. If the die is attached to the nozzle, the clamping force of the flexible carrier can be released. Now final measurements the actual placement can be performed.
If a nozzle is used for the final placement, the carrier should facilitate the nozzle in picking the die.

===== [p44-44] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 4.2 Preliminary concepts (5块) =====
--[p44]-- A brainstorm session was held to generate new ideas for the basic task of moving a die from one location to the other, with the restriction that these concepts are suitable for die to wafer assembly and flip chip. Some of the generated ideas are also found in current equipment, and others are similar to topics of former or current research projects [13] [14] [15] [16] [17]. These solutions were however not yet investigated for the current application and requirements.
--[p44]-- The proposed preliminary concepts are shown in Table 4‐2 and a more elaborate explanation is included in Appendix F. At this stage in the design process, no decisions were made yet about where to place the DOF to be actuated. One can for example move a gantry system with a nozzle over a stationary wafer table, or move a wafer table underneath a stationary nozzle. Both ideas belong to the same concept of moving a nozzle and a wafer relative to each other.
The different colored axis in Fig.
--[p44]-- 4.2 Preliminary concepts
For the task of PnP, four degrees of freedom (DOF) have to be manipulated, i.e. x,y,z and φ (Fig. 4‐1). The other two rotations are not considered. It is assumed that sufficient parallelism can be obtained between the surfaces corresponding to these rotations by some adjustment mechanism that is tuned once or periodically.
--[p44]-- For this reason the possible location of a flip unit is also indicated. The use of a flip unit depends on the configuration the die is in when it enters and exits the PnP machine.
 The wafer can enter the machine with the process layer on the top side or the underside
 There is no consensus yet if the process layer is on the top side or underside of the dies in the stacks
Fig. 4‐5 C4: Turret radial
--[p44]-- 4‐2 to Fig. 4‐11 illustrate possible locations of the DOFs. The colored axis show the possible location of the high accuracy DOF and the black arrows illustrate the non‐critical movements. Although not a requirement, it was found that for all concepts, the rotational DOF was located at the die. It is currently not clear if a so‐called flip unit is needed to turn the die over before placement. This will become clear when the Bluebird process flow becomes more final.

===== [p152-152] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > G.2 Trade­off criteria (2块) =====
--[p152]-- The criteria which positively or negatively affect the placement accuracy are grouped under the accuracy branch. The accuracy is a requirement as well and therefore, the same holds as for the throughput. The criteria stated under accuracy assess the potential of each concept to fulfill the accuracy requirement. The criteria concerning the yield of the PnP process are listed under yield. The miscellaneous branch contains criteria concerning cost and risk.
--[p152]-- G.2 Trade­off criteria
The following tree structure of trade‐off criteria was used.
The throughput branch contains the criteria affecting the amount of dies that can be processed per hour. The final throughput is a requirement and therefore should have a weighting factor of ‘infinity’. Since the actual throughput cannot be calculated at this stage, the criteria is used to evaluate the potential of each concept to fulfill the requirement.

===== [p79-79] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.2.3 Insulating layer and connection to electrodes (4块) =====
--[p79 [表]]-- This is because it may be possible to construct the actual flexible carrier from this insulator material in the future. Several commercially available films were considered, a summary is given in Table 7‐3.

--[p79]-- By creating the electrodes this way it is avoided that any air is trapped between it and the insulator material. A good surface quality of the electrodes is also assured. For these reasons it is the material of choice for the insulation layer in the test clamp.
--[p79]-- PEEK | 150 | 3.3 | 0.002
PEEK (APTIV) | 270 | 3.5 | 0.002
SiO2 [31] | 800 | 3.9
An often used film in the electronics industry is Kapton<sup>14</sup>. This polyimide tape comes in different widths and thicknesses and moreover has sufficient dielectric properties and good insulating properties. Another benefit of Kapton film is that it is possible to create a conductive layer on this film by means of physical vapor deposition (PVD), which is an in‐house process at TNO.
--[p79]-- 7.2.3 Insulating layer and connection to electrodes
The dielectric insulating layer between the electrodes and the object to be clamped prevents current from flowing to this object, whilst enabling an electric field. Even though the choice of this material is relatively free of boundary conditions for the test setup, it is preferred to have a cheap insulator material which is mechanically flexible as well.

--[p217]-- L.5.3.3 Comments
During the second set of measurements it was found that dust does in fact deteriorate the clamping force. This was found because during some measurements there was almost no clamping force at all. Visual inspection of the clamp and sample revealed that dust particles were at the interface. These measurements were excluded from the results.
The sample electrode was also damaged and repaired twice.
--[p217]-- The damage occurred when the sample was pulled up by and released from the loaded spring. It landed on the steel base plate of the stand, deforming the clamping surface (Fig. 9‐139). No clamping force was measured any more after the damage, indicating that there was a bump on the electrode. To find out if the electrode had in fact a bump it was lapped again. During lapping some material build‐up was found next to the damaged section (Fig.

===== [p25-26] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > 1.4 Problem description (8块) =====
--[p25]-- Given a stack is faulty if one die is faulty, the yield is reduced for increasing stack height and good dies are wasted as is illustrated in Fig. 113. This reduces yield and therefore increases cost [3][9]. To put this in perspective:
 A frontend processed wafer costs about \$2400
 Resulting in a cost of about \$1 per die for 2600 5x5 [mm] dies on a wafer
 A single faulty die in a 10 die stack, causes a loss of \$10
--[p25]-- The preferred process for vertical integration is die‐to‐wafer stacking[8]. This is because die‐to‐die stacking is a very flexible but time consuming process since every two dies need to be handled and aligned separately. Wafer‐to‐wafer stacking is a logical parallel assembly solution. On the other hand, with increasing stack height, the chance of a working stack decreases because faulty dies are also assembled.
--[p25]-- Die‐to‐wafer stacking provides a solution to this problem. By using only known good dies (KGDs) the chance of a working stack increases, resulting in higher yield, and therefore, lower costs (Fig. 1‐14). This flexible process, also allows different die sizes to be assembled in one stack, thereby allowing more flexibility in combining different technologies.
The technical challenge of die‐to‐wafer stacking is in the pick and place part.
--[p25]--  On average about 5% faulty 5 x 5 dies on a front‐end processes wafer with TSVs (expected value)
This \$10 loss is substantial considering the targeted cost of \$150 for assembling a single layer. Another downside to wafer‐to‐wafer stacking is that only dies of the same dimensions can be used. A mismatch in die size causes a waste of silicon for the wafers with the smaller die sizes. Considering the amount of effort put into using silicon as optimal as possible, both effects are best avoided.
--[p26]-- The required accuracy of these machines is generally in the micron or sometimes even submicron range, while the throughput is in the order of several thousand components per hour.
--[p25]-- 1.4 Problem description
To create stacks of dies for 3D‐integration there are three key possibilities, with each its advantages and disadvantages.
Die‐to‐die stacking
o Stack a single die on another single die
o High flexibility, intensive die handling
 Wafer‐to‐wafer stacking
o Stack a full wafer on another full wafer
o Low flexibility, minimal die handling
 Die‐to‐wafer stacking
o Stack single dies on a wafer
o Medium flexibility, medium die handling
--[p26]-- To assemble components together, they generally need to picked up from one location, the source, and then placed at another location, the target (Fig. 1‐15). In semiconductor industries this is generally done with a so called pick and place machine.
This pick and place process is often a challenging factor in manufacturing electronic products because these machines have to reach both high accuracies and high throughput while keeping the added cost to a minimum.
--[p26]-- Placement accuracies of down to 1 [μm] are needed for the 2016 target as a result of smaller TSV dimensions. And a placement rate of 5 dies/sec are required to make this a cost effective solution. A market analysis has shown that equipment which is capable of this performance does not yet exist (see also chapter Chapter 3).
To create a stack of dies, these dies need to be assembled together.

===== [p114-114] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.3.1 Functional diagram (2块) =====
--[p114]-- D.3.1 Functional diagram
A functional diagram of the Datacon 8800 FC Quantum has been made to clarify which processes take place in the machine, with the ultimate aim of finding the error contributions of the different routines. The functional diagram contains both calibration cycles (base calibration and auto calibration) as well as the pick and place cycle of a single gantry.
--[p114]-- The functional diagram has been structured to illustrate the error propagation throughout the different processes and also functions as a basis for the error tree.
The diagram was created based on interpretations of a video of the machine in operation, standard documentation provided with the machine, the machine’s adjuster guide[35] and running the actual auto calibration routines. The functional diagram is included in Fig. 9‐35.

===== [p47-48] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 4.3 Trade­off (6块) =====
--[p48]-- This is mostly because of the uncontrolled landing of the die, and the difficulty to implement a measurement system. The turret‐radial concept scored very well overall, but the flexible carrier concept does significantly better in terms of accuracy and yield. Because the linear guide concept does not have a clear advantage over the other concepts, and the flexible carrier concept scores better than the linear guide on all points, the linear guide concept was not invested any further.
--[p47]-- The trade‐off process itself is reported on in Appendix G. The used criteria and trade‐off results are depicted in (Fig. 4‐12 & Fig. 4‐13).
The red line indicates the average score of the preliminary concepts. The flexible carrier concept had the highest overall score. The results above have to be interpreted with caution since the trade‐off process, and especially the determination of the weighting factors is a subjective process.
--[p47]-- 4.3 Trade­off
Eight of the ten concepts were analyzed with a trade‐off process. The single nozzle concept was not analyzed because of the limitations determined in chapter Chapter 3. Concept 10, shown in Fig. 4‐11, was not analyzed because it is the subject of an ongoing PhD thesis at Delft University of Technology. This project covers the mounting of SMDs on a PCB as described by [14], [15]. Final publication was not yet available at the time of this project.
--[p47]-- The current project discusses the assembly of bare dies, which in general are more fragile than SMD components. This makes the handling and storage of the components more difficult. The applicability of the internal/external buffer concept should be re‐evaluated when final the publication is available.
A trade‐off between the remaining concepts was performed based on pair wise comparison on several weighted criteria. An Analytical Hierarchy Process Trade‐off Tool was used for this purpose.
--[p48]-- In addition this concept, the other three highest scoring ones were compared with each other to find the strengths of each (Fig. 4‐14).
The linear guide concept had less potential for highest throughput, and the wafer‐2‐wafer approach scored well in the miscellaneous category (especially on cost per placement assuming the alignment issue can be solved). However, it scored worst in the accuracy category.
--[p47]-- Despite this remark, it can safely be stated that the higher scoring concepts are the ones where the transport distance is either greatly reduced or this distance is covered by a different mechanism than the one which provides final placement accuracy. This was expected from the previous analysis (chapter Chapter 3) and confirmed here.
Of the concepts that include a turret, the ‘Turret‐radial’ concept (C4) scored best.

This master assignment considers three parts:
System analysis of a pick and place machine. Perform an analysis of a Datacon Quantum FC 8800 Quantum pick and place machine in which it becomes clear what the different contributions are to the performance of the machine. The Datacon machine is too limited in performance to fit the BlueBird roadmap.
--[p27]-- 1.4.1 Assignment
Current pick and place machines vary in performance, but the ratio between accuracy and speed is about equal (section 3.1). Within the TNO initiative BlueBird, machines required to create 3D‐ICs are investigated. For this application a pick and place machine is required which is accurate as well as fast. Typical targeted values are an accuracy of 1 [μm] with a throughput of 5 dies per second (discussed in chapter Chapter 2).

Conceptual design of a new pick and place machine. Make a conceptual design of a pick and place machine which does fit the performance required to reach the BlueBird roadmap. Besides the required accuracy and throughput (5 dies/sec @ 1 [μm]), the main input for this conceptual design is the analysis performed on the Datacon machine and the found limitations.

===== [p195-196] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.3.3 Clamping and declamping times (5块) =====
--[p195]-- K.3.3 Clamping and declamping times
To assess the clamping and declamping times one should investigate the electrical model of the capacitive clamp. For an ideal capacitive clamp, the resistance of both the air gap and the dielectric layer are infinite, and are therefore of no further interest in the electrical model. This results in the simplified model shown below. The total capacitance is equivalent of the series capacitance of the air layer and the insulation layer.
--[p195]-- The total resistance is the sum of the wire resistances.
Below is shown an example of the electric time constant for some typical values.
$$
\begin{array} { l l } { { A _ { 2 ^ { \prime } v a g e r } = \pi \cdot r ^ { 2 } \approx 2 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 3 } \Big [ m ^ { 2 } \Big ] \qquad } } & { { C _ { e q } = \frac { A \varepsilon _ { 0 } \varepsilon _ { r } } { d _ { a i r } \varepsilon _ { r } + d _ { i n s } } = 2 . 1 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 9 } \big [ F \big ] = 2 .
--[p196]-- I = \left( U / R \right) = 5 \big [ m A \big ]
$$
If the power supply is not able to deliver the needed current, the charging time will be dominated by the RC time of the power supply.

--[p195]-- This results in 95% of the clamping voltage being obtained in about 3 times the time constant, being 6.3 [μs] (Fig. 9‐104). Since the electrostatic pressure is quadratic with the voltage, 90% of the final pressure is obtained at this time instance. To realize shorter clamping times, a higher voltage can be applied during the first instance
of charging the capacitors. The maximal voltage drop over the wire resistance is 5 [V] (at t=0). This leads to a maximal current through the wires of:

--[p205]-- K.8.4 Johnson Rahbek effects and fast declamping in capacitive clamp
The buildup of JR forces can have negative effects for the capacitive clamp. Two identified problems are:
 Charge redistribution causes loss of clamping force
 Charge redistribution leads to difficult release (residual charges in air gap)
These effects are present if the voltage divider ratio caused by the resistances is very different from the voltage divider ratio caused by the capacitors.
$$

===== [p118-118] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.4.1.2.6 Thermal drift compensation (2块) =====
--[p118]-- D.4.1.2.6 Thermal drift compensation
A temperature change in the machine can occur either because of fluctuations of the ambient air temperature or by machine heat dissipation. These temperature fluctuations cause the machine to expand and contract. This affects the placement accuracy.
After the uplooking camera calibration two fixed calibration points are measured for thermal drift compensation of the ULC position. This position is important during production.
--[p118]-- Measured value: FOCT (fixed optical calibration target) position XYZ
The thermal drift is estimated at 1um/0.5hr. The thermal drift compensation method is susceptible to the following errors:
 FOCT accuracy
 G0 matching
 Gantry scales
 SC calibration
 Vibrations (external, roller guide, reaction forces)

--[p197]-- K.4.2.1 Effect of reducing electrode distance on holding force
For the effect of reducing electrode distance on holding force is different for the JR clamp as compared to the purely capacitive clamp. The effect of the changing capacitance is not important for JR forces because the voltage distribution is determined by the resistances of both capacitive layers rather than their capacitance. The reasoning below shows the effect on the exerted force when reducing the air gap height.
$$
--[p198]-- For the JR clamp the resistivity of the dielectric should be a bit lower than the interface resistivity, therefore a resistivity of about ${ 1 0 } ^ { 1 0 }$ [Ω∙cm] is preferred (for insulator thickness of about 100 μm).
--[p198]-- f = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \varepsilon \frac { V ^ { 2 } } { d ^ { 2 } } \qquad | \mathbf { \Sigma } | \quad V = , d \downarrow \Rightarrow \sqrt { f } \uparrow
$$
For a supply voltage of 40 V the voltage over the air gap is likely to reach 10 ‐ 20 [V] if the resistances in Fig. 9‐106 are of equal magnitude. The obtained pressure is now in the range of 450 – 1800 [Pa], for an air gap of 1 μm. Comparing this to Fig.
--[p198]-- 7‐2 it is obvious that much larger clamping forces can be obtained using a JR clamp as compared to a capacitive clamp for similar clamping voltages.
In designing an electrostatic clamp, the difference between the JR clamp and the CC is in the dielectric material. For the capacitive clamp a very high resistivity is preferred $\left( > 1 \cdot 1 0 ^ { 1 6 } \right.$ [Ω∙cm]).

===== [p185-186] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.3.4 Bending of die (7块) =====
--[p186 [表]]-- Table 9‐22 shows the pressure for different die thickness.
Table 9‐22 Calculated pressure to avoid peeling for a 100 [mm] radius
Thickness [μm] | 20 | 50 | 100 | 150 | 200
Pressure [Pa] | 0.20 | 3.1 | 25 | 84 | 200
--[p185]--  First, to avoid bending of the die, the clamp should be stiff enough not to deform when going around bends
 Second, to avoid release of the die from the clamp, the stiffness of the die should be small enough, and the clamping force great enough to allow the die to bend with the deforming clamp
To get an idea of the forces that come into play, a basic deformation analysis is performed on a single die. For this analysis a die thickness of 200 [μm] and a radius of 100 [mm] are assumed.

--[p186]-- 3 3 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 1 5 } \left[ m ^ { 4 } \right] } } \\ { { ~ f = q \cdot w = 1 0 \left[ P a \right] ~ } } & { { ~ \mathrm { w h e r e } ~ } } & { { ~ E \left[ 1 0 0 \right] = 1 2 9 \left[ G P a \right] } } \\ { { ~ f = q \cdot w = 1 0 \left[ P a \right] ~ } } & { { ~ E \left[ 1 1 0 \right] = 1 6 8 \left[ G P a \right] } } & { { ~ } } \\ { { ~ } } & { { ~ E \left[ 1 1 1 \right] = 1 8 6 \left[ G P a \right] } } & { { ~ } } \end{array}
$$
--[p185]-- J.3.4 Bending of die
The bending forces on the die are related to the dimensions and bending stiffness of the die, bending stiffness of the clamp, the radius of the bend and the tension in the tape. Since this is a rather complex problem and very dependent on die thickness, it will not be discussed fully but it should be investigated thoroughly when designing a tape and tape transport system. Some basic relations are however proposed.

===== [p172-173] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.2.2 Less desirable clamping mechanisms (9块) =====
--[p172]-- J.2.2 Less desirable clamping mechanisms
Mechanisms that create a form closure around the die generally obstruct both the top and bottom of the die. This makes placement more difficult since at least one DOF has to be released, to remove the die from the package, before placement can take place. Inspection and cleaning are also more difficult if not impossible. A commonly used form closure mechanism in pick and place equipment for SMD (Surface Mount Devices) is tape and reel.
--[p173]-- A liquid should be used which is compatible with silicon and have enough stiffness to hold the die in lateral direction. This liquid shouldn’t damage TSVs which are made of copper or aluminum. Also, it should be easily removed and it should not leave any residue on the die, since this could reduce conductance of the TSVs.  Furthermore, removal of the liquid should be performed in a controlled manner so the liquid does not end up on the target location (for instance the target wafer).
--[p172]-- These tapes have small pockets in which the component lies, usually the components are also glued to the inside. These pockets are usually sealed with a cover tape, which keeps the component in the pocket and seals it from the environment. The sealing tape has to be removed before the die can be removed from its pocket. This leaves the location of the die temporarily undefined in one direction. Possible problems with peeling the cover tape are illustrated below (Fig. 9‐73 ‐ Fig. 9‐75).
--[p172]-- <sup>15</sup> For rough surfaces the interlocking voids and pores determine the friction coefficient, while for very smooth surfaces electrostatic interactions between molecules become more important.
Surface tension is an effect that exists on the surface of a liquid. It causes the surface portion of a liquid to be attracted to another surface, this effect is caused by cohesion<sup>16</sup>. There are some concerns with using surface tension to hold a bare die. The first is the holding force.
--[p173]-- These potential issues make that a temporary bond using surface tension is not the primary choice.
Instead of using the surface tension of a liquid, one can also freeze the liquid to create a gripper mechanism. When a fluid is applied onto a solid it creeps into the crevices of this solid. Upon freezing the liquid, the fluid in the crevices solidifies and interlocks with these voids and pores, thereby creating a ‘mechanical lock’.
--[p173]-- Van der Waals are intermolecular attractions caused by molecules with a slight charge imbalance, molecules with slight positive and negative charges. Three types of van der Waals forces are:
 Dipoledipole interactions
 Dispersion forces
 Hydrogen bonding
These molecular interactions are generally weak, but can become significant over the bulk material.
--[p173]-- This effect is visible by the way that gecko’s stick to walls, by the millions of small hairs each ending in one thousand small pads, increasing contact area<sup>17</sup>. The main issue with van der Waals forces is that they are lack on/off behavior when relying only on material properties.
<sup>16</sup> Cohesion: The force of attraction that holds molecules of a given substance together. It is strongest in solids, less strong in liquids, and least strong in gases.
--[p173]-- Cohesion of molecules causes drops to form in liquids (as when liquid mercury is poured on a piece of glass), and causes condensing water vapor to form the droplets that make clouds. (source: www.thefreedictionary.com)
17 Source: http://www.sciencedaily.com/releases/2002/08/020828063412.htm
--[p173]-- One challenge with this concept in the scope of dies on tape is the timescale on which the liquid solidifies. This was found to be in the order of a second in a PhD research project [47]. Another potential issue is the residue left by the liquid after liquidizing it again to execute the release.
Van der Waals forces are the attractive or repulsive forces between molecules other than those due to covalent bonds or the electrostatic interaction of ions.

===== [p55-55] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 5.1.2.1 Picking of die from source wafer (4块) =====
--[p55]-- Wafer stage is moved one die pitch by an XY stage, to present next die
2. Nozzle moves down and is attached to flexible carrier
3. Flexible carrier is pressed against a die on the source wafer and clamping mechanism of carrier is activated
a. Clamping force should be greater than adhesion to tape, or
b. Adhesion of tape is reduced with external mechanism (e.g. UV tape or thermal release)
4. Die is now pushed off the tape while supported by carrier, reducing the risk of damaging the die
5.
--[p55]-- 5.1.2.1 Picking of die from source wafer
One of the benefits of the flexible carrier concept becomes evident when looking at the die pickup process. With the decreasing thickness, the dies get more compliant and more fragile. Currently the question of how to release thin dies from for example dicing tape is still pending for a solution.
--[p55]-- Nozzle moves back up and flexible carrier is free to move to next free space
The five processes described here have to be performed in the limited time frame of 200 [ms]. Using the parameters of the current Datacon wafer stage and later discussed φz‐actuator a cycle time of about 130 [ms] is achieved when taking into account 10 [ms] clamping time and 20 [ms] release time.
--[p55]-- Normal ejection pins are not useful for thin dies, especially for die thickness <30 [μm], where the dicing tape stiffness is greater than the out of plane die stiffness, see also Fig. 6‐2. A die pickup system that provides mechanical support to the die during release holds added value here. The flexible carrier can provide such a solution with the system illustrated in Fig. 5‐2.
The process is as follows.
1. Positioning
a. Flexible carrier is moved about one die pitch

===== [p171-171] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.2 Mechanisms (3块) =====
--[p171]-- J.2 Mechanisms
There are several ways to supply a holding force between die and tape (flexible carrier). These mechanisms are listed in this chapter, as well as their potential risks and difficulties. A more schematic representation of this analysis is included in Fig. 9‐85. During reading this chapter one should realize that some overlap exists between some of the discussed mechanisms.
--[p171]-- This is for because some mechanisms are more related to fundamental physics and chemistry principles, while others are more related to the application of a certain principle. For example, glue makes use of different types of adhesion like chemical adhesion, dispersive adhesion and diffusive adhesion. It was outside the scope of this project to explicitly discuss each fundamental principle and its applications. The aim is more in finding application (dies on tape) focused mechanisms.

===== [p81-82] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.3.2 Characterization of clamp (5块) =====
--[p81]-- The alternate solution was to measure the capacitance of the created clamp. Next, the average relative air gap was determined with the following formula.
$$
C = \varepsilon \cdot \frac { A } { l } { \Rightarrow } l = \frac { C } { \varepsilon \cdot A }
$$
$$
\varepsilon = 8 . 8 5 4 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 1 2 } \Big [ F _ { m } \Big ]
$$
The relative thickness of the insulator layer should be deducted  from the average relative air gap to obtain the actual average air gap.
--[p81]-- This was partially due to clamp imperfections and partially due to sample electrode imperfections. A high quality 200 [nm] flat electrode was
placed on the whole clamp to characterize its contribution to the air gap. It was found that the air gap between flat electrode and clamp was 3.4 [μm], the full area of the clamp was covered. This was slightly more than aimed for (which was 1[μm]) but still good enough for the experiments.
--[p82]-- In a project meeting following these experiments it was stated that the bumps on the dies will have a height of about 3 [μm], which was a change from the original 1 [μm]. Because of this change the results obtained in the pressure (force) measurements approach the targeted values well.
--[p81]-- This is because the dielectric properties of the insulator affect the electric field.
$$
l = d _ { a i r } + \frac { d _ { i n s } } { \varepsilon _ { r } } \Rightarrow d _ { a i r } = l - \frac { 2 5 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 6 } } { 3 . 4 } [ m ]
$$
The capacitance measurements as discussed in app. L.5 resulted in an average air gap between sample and clamp of 4.6±0.3 [μm], depending on the location of the sample on the clamp.
--[p81]-- 7.3.2 Characterization of clamp
The holding pressure that can be accomplished with an electrostatic clamp is heavily dependent on the air gap between the clamp electrodes and the sample electrode. For this reason it was important to measure this parameter. The first solution was to measure the surface topography of both with an interferometer. However, the surfaces were found not reflective enough to get good measurements.

===== [p206-206] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.8.4.1 Deterioration of clamping force (2块) =====
--[p206]-- K.8.4.1 Deterioration of clamping force
The capacitive clamp with parameters as stated in Table 9‐26 deterioration of clamping forces is visible. The initial charging is shown in Fig. 9‐115. The deterioration on a much longer time scale is visualized in Fig. 9‐117.
A RC time of about 1400 [s] is observed (about 23 minutes). Approximately the same RC time is obtained for the simplified circuit of just the air gap capacitance and resistance.
$$
--[p206]-- R C _ { t i m e } = R _ { t o t } \cdot C _ { t o t } = R _ { a i r } \cdot C _ { a i r } = 1 1 0 0 \big [ s \big ]
$$
This indicates that a small current flows over the air gap, reducing capacitor charge and therefore holding pressure. Since the air gap resistance is dominant, the RC time can be increased by using a larger air gap.

===== [p154-154] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > G.2.13 Cost per placement (2块) =====
--[p154]-- G.2.13 Cost per placement
Last but not least, the cost involved with placing a die. The cost of placing a die consists basically of two parts. First, the part associated with the actual resources needed for placing a single die, the variable cost. For example, power, wear (maintenance) and disposed material. Second, there are also cost that cannot be directly related to a die, the fixed cost. For example, acquisition, housing, labor and periodic maintenance.
$$
--[p154]-- C _ { t o t a l } = C _ { f i x e d } + { n _ { d i e s } } \cdot C _ { \nu a r i a b l e }
$$
$$
C _ { d i e } = \frac { C _ { f i x e d } } { n _ { d i e s } } + C _ { \nu a r i a b l e }
$$

===== [p136-136] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > Appendix F Preliminary concepts (2块) =====
--[p136]-- Appendix F Preliminary concepts
This appendix gives an overview of the concepts that were created, and are briefly discussed in chapter 0. The workings of each concept are explained, the cycle time is estimated and some advantages and disadvantages are stated.
Any pick and place machine basically performs three operations
 Pickup die from source
 Transport die from source to target location
 Place die on target
Of these three processes only the last one requires high accuracy.
--[p136]-- The other two do not and are generally high speed processes to reduce cycle time. This appendix concerns the preliminary concepts focused on fast component supply.

===== [p160-160] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > H.3.3.1 Die to stack (3块) =====
--[p160]-- The measurement of the neighboring stacks brings with it the risk of error propagation along stacks. If one die is placed incorrectly, its error propagates to the stacks when this die is used as reference.
The benefit of this method is the extremely small measurement loop, making the system very robust against for example thermal expansion and vibrations. An obvious difficulty with this system is that the dies are very thin, making it very difficult to aim a laser spot on it.
--[p160]-- H.3.3.1 Die to stack
Assuming the dicing process is chosen such that the relative position of the IC on the die is known and that the sides of the die are reflective, this surface can be used for interferometry. One X, Y ,φ measurement is obtained for the die, and one for the stack. Or a relative measurement of both is obtained straight away.
One set of interferometers is aimed at the die, and one set at neighboring stacks. This way the measurement loop is minimized.
--[p160]-- And current dicing techniques are not accurate enough to create a reference for pick and place. The dicing street location uncertainty is larger than allowable placement error. Also, the surface quality of the sides of the die are not necessarily smooth and reflective.

===== [p28-28] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > 2.1.1 Dimensions of dies and stacks (3块) =====
--[p28]-- This results in the possibility to combine a large number of technologies and applications in one stack. This, amongst other reasons is why it is expected that stacks will reach up to about 14 layers in 2016.
--[p28]-- 2.1.1 Dimensions of dies and stacks
The developed process needs to be compatible with almost all die sizes, ranging from large dies $( 2 5 0 ~ \mathsf { m m } ^ { 2 } )$ to small dies (0.025 mm<sup>2</sup>). According to the roadmap presented in Table 1‐1, the thickness of the dies is expected to be reduced to 20 ‐ 50 [μm] in 2016, resulting from the capabilities of the TSV creation techniques (aspect ratio related).
--[p28]-- Due to recent advancements in these techniques, the 50 [μm] thickness is most likely. Since throughput and accuracy could be affected by die size, the benchmark for this master thesis are 5x5 [mm] dies with a thickness of 50 [μm].
TNO focuses on heterogeneous stacks, which basically means that different types and sizes of dies can be expected in a single stack (Fig. 2‐3). Thus large and small dies of different technologies (e.g. logic and memory) are stacked on top of each other.

===== [p164-165] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > I.2.3 φ­Z actuator (5块) =====

It also has to be able to rotate with an accuracy of about 0.1 [mrad] (see appendix B.1.2). In order to cover the z‐stroke in a limited time, values of the order as stated are needed to obtain a setup time of < 50 [ms]:

--[p165 [表]]-- The actuator that comes closest to the requirements is the Integrated Electro‐Mechanical Actuator (or INEMA), which is currently being developed in a research project collaboration of Assembléon, Prodirve, Technobis, Tecnotion and TNO Science and Industry [18].
As the INEMA actuator fits the requirements best it is currently the actuator of choice for the placement system of the newly developed PnP concept.

===== [p222-222] Appendix L Experimental investigation of electrostatic tape > L.6.2 Conclusions (2块) =====
--[p222]-- Although it looked like a good combination with the aluminum electrodes, the actual insulating performance of this layer is found questionable. Other opportunities exist in the use of some organic dielectric materials that can be deposited. Because of the good performance of the Kapton layer, this was not further investigated for the current project. These layers should however be considered when starting the actual flexible carrier design.
--[p222]-- L.6.2 Conclusions
Clearly, the ${ \mathsf { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ layer was not suitable for the clamp. If the breakdown occurred only due to imperfections in the $\mathsf { S i O } _ { 2 }$ layer or due to mechanical wear of this layer is unknown, either would be a reason not to use this material as dielectric in the current application.
As an alternative to the $\mathsf { S i O } _ { 2 }$ layer an aluminum oxide layer was proposed.

===== [p158-158] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > H.3.1.1 Opportunities (1块) =====
--[p158]-- H.3.1.1 Opportunities
To further improve the accuracy of this system, the downward motion of the nozzle should have very high repeatability or it should be corrected for XY. Two suggestions are:
 Use vision in the loop to measure nozzle or die motion during placement and correct for this o To track die, good depth of field is preferred (small Numerical Aperture)
 Use accelerometers attached to nozzle or phi‐z actuator and to wafer stage to measure relative motion and correct for this

===== [p78-78] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.2.1 Schematic design of clamp (2块) =====
--[p78]-- The base material provides the mechanical interface to the test setup and also makes the clamp easy to handle. More important, the base material was chosen very flat (sub‐micron) since any imperfections here cause an increased average air gap. The layer on top of the base material is a nonconductive adhesive. The electrodes on the bottom of the insulation material are attached to the base by this adhesive.
--[p78]-- 7.2.1 Schematic design of clamp
For the design of the clamp, it was attempted to use materials and processes that would be available in creating an actual commercial flexible carrier. Therefore, a simple and inexpensive design was aimed for. The clamp should also be easy to handle and provide an interface that allows electrical and mechanical connections to the test setup. ===== [p158-158] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > H.3.1 Co­location with two camera’s (2块) =====
--[p158]-- H.3.1 Co­location with two camera’s
Using a beam splitter (e.g. semi –reflecting mirror), the image of both the die and substrate can be captured on a single image processor. By superimposing the images of both location a measurement of their offset can be obtained. A second camera is used to look at another fiducial. This way the angular error can also be measured since obtaining this measurement from the xy camera is not possible due to the limited field of view.
--[p158]-- To make this concept work successfully, the optical length from camera to die and from camera to substrate should be equal. For this reason it is important to have an accurate height measurement of both the substrate relative to the beam splitter and the die to the beam splitter.
Two drawbacks of this system are the large required z‐stroke of the phi‐z actuator and the blind placement stroke, since no measurement can be taken when the nozzle moves down.

===== [p193-194] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.3.2.2 Effect of reducing electrode distance on holding force (6块) =====
--[p193]-- \begin{array} { l } { { C _ { 1 } = \varepsilon \displaystyle \frac { \cal A } { { \cal d } _ { 1 } } \qquad \mid \mid \quad { \cal d } _ { 1 } \not \downarrow \Rightarrow C _ { 1 } \uparrow } } \\ { { { \cal C } _ { 2 } = { \cal C } _ { 2 } } } \\ { { { \cal V } _ { 1 } = \displaystyle \frac { { \cal C } _ { 2 } } { { \cal C } _ { 1 } + { \cal C } _ { 2 } } { \cdot { \cal V } _ { s } } \quad \mid \mid \quad { \cal C } _ { 1 } \uparrow \Rightarrow { \cal V } _ { 1 } \downarrow \quad \mid \mid \quad { \cal d } _ { 1 } \not \downarrow \Rightarrow { \cal V } _ { 1 } \downarrow } } \end{array}
$$
The effect on the electrostatic pressure is not directly obvious since the relations are not linear
$$
--[p193]-- f = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \varepsilon \frac { V _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } } { { d _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } } \qquad | \ | \quad V _ { 1 } \downarrow , d _ { 1 } \downarrow \Rightarrow f ?
$$
The figures below illustrate the pressure dependence on the variation of the air gap height and insulation thickness. The following parameters were used.
$$
--[p193]-- \begin{array} { r l r l } & { \varepsilon _ { \scriptscriptstyle 0 } = 8 , 8 5 4 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 1 2 } \Big [ \underline { { F } } \Big / \underset { m } { \Big ] } \quad } & & { 1 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 7 } \leq d _ { a i r } \leq 1 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 5 } \big [ m \big ] } \\ & { \varepsilon _ { \scriptscriptstyle r } = 3 , 4 \quad } & & { 5 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 6 } \leq d _ { i n s } \leq 5 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 5 } \big [ m \big ] } \\ & { V _ { \scriptscriptstyle s } = 5 \big [ { V } \big ] \quad } & & { R _ { a i r } = \infty } \\ & { } & & { R _ { i s o } = \infty } \end{array}
$$
The figure above shows an decreased dependence of pressure on air gap height for increased insulation thickness.
--[p193]-- This affects the voltage distribution over the voltage divider, as is illustrated in the example below. In this series network, with one static (insulator) and one varying capacity (air gap), the following relations hold.
$$
--[p193]-- K.3.2.2 Effect of reducing electrode distance on holding force
As stated before, the electrostatic pressure equation contains a quadratic term for the electrode distance. The capacitance of the clamp is however also dependant on this distance. If one simply enters a static capacitance in the equations, the electrostatic pressure is overestimated. The reason for this is the increasing capacitance with decreasing distance between the capacitor plates.
--[p194]-- The increased insulation thickness however also result in a higher voltage required to obtain the required pressure. So, for a chosen air gap, there is a tradeoff between insulation thickness and its dielectric constant, the required voltage and the linearity of the pressure for changing air gap height.

===== [p75-75] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.1.2.2 Die with TSVs, bulk Si side (5块) =====
--[p75]-- Because the exact path of the electrostatic field is unknown and the effect of this field on the IC is a risk, this topic should be further investigated. A full analysis of the effect of the electrostatic clamp on the IC is not relevant for now, since this is out of the scope of the current project.
--[p75]-- Now it has been identified that there is a possibility that the electric field of the clamp could create a potentia over oxide layers inside the IC, the effects of this should be considered. An actual IC consists of many serial and parallel connections of transistors, capacitors, resistances, diodes and other components. When these components are placed in series, they act as voltage dividers.
--[p75]-- 7.1.2.2 Die with TSVs, bulk Si side
The situation changes a bit for a die with TSVs, since these vertical connections through the body of dies conduct electricity (Fig. 7‐8). This brings with it the risk that the electric field of the bipolar clamp is guided into the process layer. One side note here is that these TSVs are insulated from the bulk material, so charge will not flow between them. Therefore, charge in the bulk material will remain in the bulk material.
--[p75]-- When the design of the electrostatic clamp reaches a more mature stage, it is recommended to consult IDMs on their vision for the proposed solution and to perform experiments to find the limits of what fields the IC actually can handle.
--[p75]-- Because of the large amount of components on an IC, it is unlikely that the full potential of the clamp is over a single oxide layer. Also, to charge the capacitors to their breakdown voltage, it has to be supplied with current. The limited current that is caused by the bipolar capacitive clamp makes it even more unlikely that oxide breakdown will occur.

===== [p77-77] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.1.4 Clamping and declamping times (7块) =====
--[p77]-- \tau _ { _ { R C } } = \left( R _ { _ { w i r e } } + \frac { R _ { _ { d i e } } } { 2 } \right) \cdot \frac { 1 } { \left( \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { C _ { _ { i n s } } } + \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { C _ { _ { a i r } } } \right) } = R _ { _ { t o t } } \cdot C _ { t o t }
$$
Since the wire resistance is much lower than the die resistance, it is neglected. With the typical values as stated in Table 7‐2, a RC time constant of 0.5[μs] is obtained.
--[p77]-- The negative charge for the top plate of the air gap capacitance is a result of the current that flows through the die. The same process holds for the side of the negative voltage source. The RC time constant is determined by looking at one side of the bipolar clamp (right side Fig. 7‐13).
$$
--[p77]-- The clamping and declamping times are dependent on the electrical RC time constant. For a bipolar purely capacitive clamp (without JR forces), the simplified electrical scheme is shown in Fig. 7‐13.
During the buildup of clamping forces, the positive voltage source charges the bottom plate of the insulator capacitance (left side Fig. 7‐13). The top plate of this capacitance is charged by removing negative charge from the air gap capacitance bottom plate.
--[p77]-- Insulator resistivity | $\boldsymbol { 1 \cdot 1 0 } ^ { 1 7 } \left[ \boldsymbol { \Omega \cdot } \boldsymbol { \mathsf { c m } } \right]$ | $C _ { a i r } \left( x 2 \right)$ | $2 2 0 [ \mathsf { p F } ]$
Insulator thickness | $2 5 [ \mu \mathrm { m } ]$ | $\tau _ { C C }$ | 0.5 [μs]
$\tau _ { J R \_ a i r }$ | 150 [min]
$\underline { { \tau _ { J R \ i n s } } }$ | 16.5 [day]
--[p77 [表]]-- This means that 95% of the clamping force is obtained and removed in about 1.5 [μs]. Typical RC times for JR force build‐up in a capacitive clamp are also stated in Table 7‐2.
Table 7‐2 Typical values for capacitive clamp parameters, bipolar clamp
Parameter | Value | Circuit | Value
Die size | $2 5 [ \mathsf { m m } ^ { 2 } ]$ | $R _ { d i e }$ | $3 6 \cdot 1 0 ^ { 3 } \left[ \Omega \right]$
--[p77]-- 7.1.4 Clamping and declamping times
The capacitive electrostatic clamp works by redistribution of the charge over the capacitors. This allows very short clamping and declamping times since leakage currents over the capacitors are not required. These small leakage currents are however required for the build‐up of Johnson Rahbek forces. The clamping mechanism based on these Johnson Rahbek effect and its parasitic behavior on the capacitive clamp are further discussed in app K.4 and app. K.8.

Dielectric constant insulator | 3.4 | $C _ { i n s } \left( x 2 \right)$ | $3 0 [ \mathsf { p F } ]$

===== [p182-185] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.3.3 Interactions with air (14块) =====
--[p185]-- F _ { d r a g 2 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \rho _ { a i r } \cdot \nu ^ { 2 } \cdot A _ { l i f t } \cdot C _ { l } \cdot \sin { ( \alpha ) } = 1 . 1 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 6 } \left[ N \right] \Rightarrow f _ { d r a g 2 } = 0 . 4 6 \left[ P a \right]
$$
The figures below illustrate the change of lift and drag with changing dimensions and angle of the lifted part of the die. The marked points illustrate the values resulting from the calculations above.
--[p183]-- F _ { d } = - \frac { 1 } { 2 } \rho _ { f l u i d } \cdot \nu ^ { 2 } \cdot A \cdot C _ { d } \cdot \frac { \nu } { \left| \nu \right| }
$$
Where, $\rho _ { f I u i d }$ is the density of the fluid, v is the speed of the object relative to the fluid, A is the reference area the force is acting on, $C _ { d }$ is the drag coefficient and the last term is the unit vector indicating the direction of the velocity. The drag coefficient is dependent on the shape of the die.
--[p183]-- For a square, the $C _ { d }$ is typically $1 . 0 5 ( ^ { 1 9 } )$ For the case where the top part determines the drag, the drag coefficient is dependent on the flow regime. Typically $C _ { d }$ is about 0.005 for turbulent flow and down to 0.001 for laminar flow. Since the thin die mostly resembles a flat plate, only the drag force due to the friction created by laminar, transitional and turbulent boundary layers is assessed (Fig. 9‐89).
--[p184]-- The lift force is perpendicular to the plane on the airfoil. The thin airfoil theory does not account for the stall of the airfoil which usually occurs at an angle of attack of about ${ 1 0 ^ { \circ } }$ to $1 5 ^ { \circ } .$ . Therefore it is only valid for small angles. When decomposing the lift force, it is obvious that the actual lift pulling the airfoil up is different from the calculated lift force. Also, the lift force causes a force component opposite the direction of motion, or drag.
--[p183]-- Besides drag, the die also experiences lift when for example a side or corner of the die is slightly elevated from the clamp. The lift exerted on the die is estimated with a formula similar to the one used for drag.
$$
F _ { l } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \rho _ { f l u i d } \cdot \nu ^ { 2 } \cdot A \cdot C _ { l } \cdot \frac { \nu } { \left| \nu \right| }
$$
19 Source: http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drag\_coefficient
--[p185]-- 5 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 6 } \left[ N \right] \Rightarrow f _ { d r a g 1 } = 2 . 5 \left[ P a \right]
$$
$$
C _ { l } = 2 \pi \cdot \alpha = { \frac { \pi ^ { 2 } } { 9 } }
$$
$$
F _ { l i f t } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \rho _ { a i r } \cdot \nu ^ { 2 } \cdot A _ { l i f t } \cdot C _ { l } \cdot \cos \left( \alpha \right) = 6 . 5 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 6 } \left[ N \right] \Rightarrow f _ { l i f t } = 2 . 6 \left[ P a \right]
$$
$$
--[p185]-- F _ { d r a g 1 \_ t o p } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \rho _ { a i r } \cdot \nu ^ { 2 } \cdot A _ { d i e } \cdot C _ { _ { d \_ t o p } } \cdot \frac { \nu } { \left| \nu \right| } = 3 . 1 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 6 } \left[ N \right] \Longrightarrow f _ { d r a g 1 } = 0 . 1 3 \left[ P a \right]
$$
$$
F _ { d r a g 1 \_ f r o n t } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \rho _ { a i r } \cdot \nu ^ { 2 } \cdot A _ { f r o n t } \cdot C _ { d \_ f r o n t } \cdot \frac { \nu } { | \nu | } = 2 .
--[p184]-- The lift coefficient is dependent on the shape of the cross section of the die parallel to the flow. For simplicity the ‘thin airfoil theory<sup>20</sup>’ is assumed. This theory relates the angle of the airfoil relative to the flow to the lift coefficient.
$$
C _ { \iota } = 2 \pi \cdot \alpha \quad \mathrm { f o r } \qquad \left( \alpha < \frac { \pi } { 1 2 } \right)
$$
Where α is the angle of the airfoil relative to the flow in radians.
--[p185]-- The calculations above are only first estimates to a very complicated problem in which the domains of mechanics, aerodynamics and electrostatics are combined. To find actual requirements for carrier design, it is advised to perform multi‐physics analysis. This can for instance be done with Ansys.
--[p184]-- This is illustrated in the figure below.

--[p182]-- J.3.3 Interactions with air
During movement as well as during stand still, the die interacts with the moving air around it, the latter will be neglected for now. When the die is moved, the air around it will exert both drag and lift forces on the die. The
drag is created on the front part of the die, perpendicular to the plane of the die, as well as on the top part of the die, parallel to the plane of the die (Fig. 9‐88). Either way, the drag is estimated with the formula below.
$$

===== [p120-120] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.4.2.3.2 Camera lens errors (2块) =====
--[p120]-- Also, the misalignment of the optical and mechanical axis causes a position error dependant on the focal distance and the image being out of focus.
Besides the errors discussed above, lens distortions also contribute to the placemen error. These are not discussed here since these are difficult to estimate.
--[p120]-- D.4.2.3.2 Camera lens errors
In this calibration routine the image to be processed is centered on the lens. Only its position, rather than position and shape, is important. Therefore image distortions due to imperfections in the lens are not important here. The error considered here is due to the defocus of the lens. If the object is not completely in focus it may appear larger or smaller on the imaging chip.

===== [p179-180] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.2.3.3.4 Local pressure chambers (9块) =====
--[p179]-- Furthermore, the carrier should resist elongation caused by pulling on the carrier by the carrier transport system. While it should also resist compression stresses created by the tape tending to bend at the vacuum chambers, where the compression stresses at the underside of the tape are greater than at the top.
Based on the estimated required holding pressure, an estimation can be made for the required membrane parameters.
--[p179]-- First, to be less sensitive to pressure loss, the internal cavity should be as large as possible, so preferably die size up to die pitch, for now an area of 5x5 [mm] is assumed. The tape itself should not be too thick, so a cavity height of 2 [mm] is assumed. Given the fact that it should be possible to over pressurize the tape to release the die, about half this height is available to create holding pressure. The required and possible stiffness are now estimated by simple 2D calculations.
$$
--[p179]-- The pressure is then created by the stiffness of the system which wants to push it back into its original shape. An example of possible implementation is using the carrier material itself as a membrane (Fig. 6‐8), which when pushed upon releases some air from the chamber inside the carrier. The pressure is then created by the stiffness of the membrane, trying to push the membrane into its original shape.
--[p180]-- 1 6 \cdot 2 \cdot 1 0 ^ { s } \cdot \left( \frac { 5 \cdot 1 0 ^ { 3 } \cdot \left( 1 0 0 \cdot 1 0 ^ { 6 } \right) ^ { 3 } } { 1 2 } \right) } \end{array} } \\ & { \begin{array} { r l } & { K _ { s p e r a s s } = \frac { 1 6 E \cdot 1 } { \underset { m } { \ell } } = \frac { 1 6 E \cdot 1 } { \underset { l _ { s = 0 } ^ { 3 } } { \longrightarrow } \underset { m } { \ell } } } \end{array} } \end{array}
$$
--[p180]-- 1 \left[ \overset { N } { \underset { m } { \ell } } \right] } \\ & { K _ { v \mathrm { e n t a s s } } = \frac { 1 6 E \cdot 1 } { \underset { m } { \ell } } = \frac { 1 6 E \cdot 1 } { \underset { m } { \ell } } \frac { \mathrm { w e n t a s s } \cdot \underset { \mathrm { r i s s t e n s t } } { \ell } \cdot \underset { m } { \ell } ^ { 3 } } { \underset { l _ { s = 0 } ^ { 2 } } { \longrightarrow } } .
--[p179]-- To release the die the membrane can be pushed inward even more, creating an overpressure inside the carrier, pushing the die off.
The main limitation of local pressure chambers is, that any leak will reduce the holding force over time, finally releasing the die. Also, the carrier should be stiff enough to create the local vacuum by its elasticity. Despite of the elasticity that is required to obtain and maintain the vacuum, the carrier should also be flexible enough to go around bends.
--[p180]-- \begin{array} { r l } & { f _ { e s u } = 4 \left[ P u \right] } \\ & { \begin{array} { r l } & { 1 } \\ & { 4 = 0 . 0 0 5 ^ { 2 } \left[ m ^ { 2 } \right] } \\ & { F = f _ { s h \omega } \cdot A = 0 . 1 \left[ m N \right] } \\ & { d _ { a s u \omega a s a } = 1 \left[ m m \right] } \end{array} } \\ & { \begin{array} { r l } & { K _ { v _ { \mathrm { e n d e s s } } } = \frac { F } { d _ { a s \mathrm { e n t a s s } } } = \frac { 1 e - 4 } { 1 6 - 3 } = 0 .
--[p180]-- Clearly, the required stiffness is much greater than the obtainable stiffness for a typical foil with a thickness of 100 [μm] and an approximated modulus of elasticity of 2 [GPa].
--[p179]-- J.2.3.3.4 Local pressure chambers
The last concept with vacuum does not rely on a vacuum pump. Instead of using a continuous supply of vacuum, one can create a local vacuum inside the carrier by using the elasticity of the carrier material itself. The carrier elasticity could be used to create and hold the vacuum at specified locations in the carrier. To create a pressure difference, the carrier system should be able to alter the internal volume (of air) inside it.

===== [p116-116] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.4.1.1.1 Error sources (1块) =====
--[p116]-- D.4.1.1.1 Error sources
During the base calibration procedures there is no motion in the wafer stage or flip units. Also, the gantry is operated at low speed. This leads to the case that there are virtually no vibrations introduced by reaction force. The external vibrations due to floor vibrations and pump/fan vibrations are however not to be omitted. The effect of these vibrations on the ‘bond head – frame’ distance should be modeled or measured.
Other error sources are the accuracy of the gantry scales and glass plate. Also the resolution of the camera and its lens errors are important.

===== [p161-161] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > H.4.1 Additional possibilities (2块) =====
--[p161]-- H.4.1 Additional possibilities
For the flexible carrier concept it was proposed to have the XY alignment located at the wafer stage. For reduced reaction forces and thereby increased accuracy it is beneficial to move as little mass as possible. Therefore it is suggested to place an additional, small stroke – high bandwidth XY stage at the nozzle.
--[p161]-- This XY stage can for instance be used for the final alignment stroke or even for small adjustments during placement to correct for small nozzle motions.

===== [p192-193] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.3.2.1 Electrostatic force, alternate explanation (5块) =====
--[p192]-- f _ { 1 } = - \frac { 1 } { 2 } \cdot \varepsilon _ { 0 } \cdot \frac { V _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } } { d _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } } = - \frac { 1 } { 2 } \cdot \varepsilon _ { 0 } \cdot \left( \frac { C _ { 2 } } { C _ { 1 } + C _ { 2 } } \right) ^ { 2 } \cdot \frac { V _ { s } ^ { 2 } } { d _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } }
$$
Substituting $\mathsf C _ { 1 }$ and $\mathsf C _ { 2 }$ results in
$$
--[p193]-- { \begin{array} { l } { f _ { 0 } = { \frac { - \varepsilon _ { 0 } \cdot V _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } } { 2 d _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } } } } \\ { f _ { 1 } = { \frac { - \varepsilon _ { 0 } \cdot V _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } } { 2 d _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } } } { \Biggl ( } { \frac { \varepsilon _ { r } \cdot d _ { 1 } } { d _ { 2 } + \varepsilon _ { r } \cdot d _ { 1 } } } { \Biggr ) } ^ { 2 } { \Biggl \{ } { \frac { f _ { 1 } } { f _ { 0 } } } = \left( { \frac { \varepsilon _ { r } \cdot d _ { 1 } } { d _ { 2 } + \varepsilon _ { r } \cdot d _ { 1 } } } \right) ^ { 2 } = { \frac { 1 } { \left( 1 + { \frac { d _ { 2 } } { \varepsilon _ { r } \cdot d _ { 1 } } } \right) ^ { 2 } } } } \end{array} }
$$
$$
--[p192]-- f _ { 1 } = \frac { - \varepsilon _ { 0 } \cdot V _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } } { 2 d _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } } \Bigg ( \frac { \varepsilon _ { \boldsymbol { r } } \cdot d _ { 1 } } { d _ { 2 } + \varepsilon _ { \boldsymbol { r } } \cdot d _ { 1 } } \Bigg ) ^ { 2 } = \frac { - \varepsilon _ { 0 } \cdot \varepsilon _ { \boldsymbol { r } } ^ { 2 } \cdot V _ { s } ^ { 2 } } { 2 \left( d _ { 2 } + \varepsilon _ { \boldsymbol { r } } \cdot d _ { 1 } \right) ^ { 2 } }
$$
This is the same result as obtained before.
The effect of the dielectric (insulator) layer is evaluated as follows.
$$
--[p192]-- K.3.2.1 Electrostatic force, alternate explanation
One could imagine adding conductive plates between the air layer and insulator. The presence of the voltage divider is now more obvious.
$$
C _ { 1 } = \varepsilon _ { 0 } \frac { A } { d _ { 1 } }
$$
$$
C _ { 2 } = \varepsilon _ { 0 } \varepsilon _ { r } { \frac { A } { d _ { 2 } } }
$$
$$
V _ { 1 } = \frac { C _ { 2 } } { C _ { 1 } + C _ { 2 } } \cdot V _ { s }
$$
$$
--[p193]-- f _ { 1 } = \frac { f _ { 0 } } { \left( 1 + \displaystyle \frac { d _ { 2 } } { \varepsilon _ { r } \cdot d _ { 1 } } \right) ^ { 2 } }
$$
So, when increasing insulator thickness $( d _ { 2 } )$ , the electrostatic force is reduced. The highest electrostatic force is obtained for a thin layer, with a high relative permittivity.
