--[p219]-- The most important rows here are the maximum obtained values. These values are more important since they give a better estimate of the clamping pressure. This is because of the effects dust on the interface and because of the difficulty of pulling the sample of in a horizontal position.
--[p219]-- Often a reduced holding force was measured because the sample was released on one side first (peeling).,This reduced the holding force gradually and therefore the reduced the maximum measured holding force of the clamp. This translated in a lower electrical pressure than was actually present (random measurement error). As peeling cannot cause a higher clamping force the spread caused by this effect has a non‐Gaussian distribution.
--[p219]-- The average value of the force measurements therefore holds limited value. The times the sample was pulled of level probably registered a higher clamping force that was more representative for the actual holding pressure, for that reason these values were used.
The downside of using the maximum value is that increased clamping pressure due to charging of the interface is also visible, see for example the high clamping pressures obtained for very low voltages in Table 9‐35.

===== [p218-218] Appendix L Experimental investigation of electrostatic tape > L.5.4 Force measurements third set (4块) =====
--[p218]-- L.5.4 Force measurements third set
The lessons learned from the first set of measurements were that it is important to clean the clamp and sample surface in between measurements for two reasons:
 Charge buildup at interface  higher clamping force
 Dust buildup at interface  lower clamping force
For this reason more measurements were performed and the clamp and sample were cleaned often with alcohol. This removed the charge and dust at the interface.
--[p218]-- This was done to reduce the chance of the sample electrode coming loose from the spring and being damaged. It also reduced the chance of the sample electrode peeling loose from the clamp with reduced force instead of being released at once.
The sample was attached to the clamp again for just a few seconds before pulling it off.
--[p218]-- By using this a short interval it was guaranteed that the Johnson Rahbek effects did not interfere  with the measurements for the capacitive clamp as discussed in section K.8.
--[p218]-- The removal of charge was also confirmed. If the clamp showed excessive clamping force, even with the clamp short circuited, the clamp and sample were cleaned with alcohol. After placing the sample on the short circuited clamp again, the holding force diminished. The measurements were performed in two sessions, first ±80 [V] to ±180 [V], next the range was expanded to ±20 [V]  and ±275[V].
Furthermore, for these measurements the use of the compliant spring was omitted.

===== [p62-62] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 6.1 Tapes in back­end (5块) =====
--[p62]-- The used tapes are available in many different shapes and sizes. Flat tapes with adhesives exist or with cavities for components to fit into. Tapes for bare dies also exist [19]. These tapes are sometimes covered by a second tape to avoid the components falling out of the cavities if the adhesive force between tape and component does not suffice. Contamination is also avoided by using cover tapes. Despite the large variety in tapes, they have one thing in common.
--[p62]-- Concerns were also raised on the subject of contamination. Particles of the glue could potentially stay attached to the die, or even to the TSVs, resulting in poor conduction of electricity. Within TNO a team is currently investigating the possibility of creating a very low residue, thermally switchable adhesive [20]. Early results look promising, the adhesive has for example less residue than commercial alternatives.
--[p62]-- The adhesive is however far from a finished product, and therefore not a solution to the current issue. To find a suitable solution, the requirements were investigated first.
--[p62]-- 6.1 Tapes in back­end
The use of components on a ‘flexible carrier’ or tape is not a new concept in back‐end semiconductor technology, it is even very common for SMD components (not bare dies for die bonders) to be delivered to the PnP machines on a tape. Most SMT PnP machines use tape and reel systems to supply a large range of components to the PnP gantry system. Fig. 6‐1 shows an illustration of a tape and reel system in a typical SMT PnP machine.
--[p62]-- All tapes used in tape and reel systems in back‐end use some kind of chemical adhesive (glue) to keep the component attached to the tape.
For the application of attaching bare dies to a flexible carrier, using a chemical adhesive is not necessarily the best solution. These tapes are most likely machine consumables, increasing cost per placement (CPP). This is less of an issue for finished products (packaged dies) as compared to single dies because of the higher value of the finished products.

===== [p98-98] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > B.1.1 TSV overlap (5块) =====
--[p98]-- A rule of thumb is that the position error should be less than 25% of the contact diameter.

--[p98]-- B.1.1 TSV overlap
The electrical interconnection between the stacked dies is provided by Through‐Silicon Vias (TSVs). During pick and place it is essential that the TSVs of the stacked dies are aligned properly. A rule of thumb is that the overlap of the TSVs is 70% of the contact area, otherwise the connection between the stacked TSVs can lead to additional resistances in the electronics circuit. ===== [p19-20] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > 1.2 3D­integration (5块) =====
--[p19]-- <sup>3</sup> Moore's law is a prediction made by Gordon Moore in 1965 and describes a long‐term trend in the history of computing hardware, in which the number of transistors that can be placed inexpensively on an integrated circuit has doubled approximately every two years. [53]
<sup>4</sup> IPC is a global trade association dedicated to furthering the competitive excellence and financial success of its members, who are participants in the electronics industry [56]
--[p19]-- 1.2 3D­integration
The ever increasing demands for more functionality, higher performance and smaller size of electronics have driven the industry make use of the $3 ^ { \mathsf { r d } }$ dimension, by stacking dies or packages. This reduces total size even further and increases performance and functionality per surface area [3]. Several techniques exist today to accomplish this.
--[p20]-- One currently used process is stacking bare dies and creating interconnections with wire bonds to a single package (Fig. 1‐6, Fig. 1‐7). These wire bonds can only be placed at the perimeter of the die. Thereby limiting the number of connections that can be made compared when using the full surface area of the die. These wire bonds are generally very fragile and need to be made individually, resulting in increased cost per chip for increasing number of wire bonds.
--[p20]-- However limitations in the number of connections and the increasing cost with number of connections stimulates the search for alternatives for future applications. The solution to a very similar problem was found by IBM in the 1960’s and found its way to the market since the 1970’s [4]. This solution is described in the following section 1.2.1.
--[p20]-- The challenge and main driver in the electronics industry and therefore also the market of 3D‐integration is cost. With the expected amount of interconnections to a single die in a stack reaching around one thousand in the near future, the cost of wire bonds becomes increasingly important. Wire bonding stacked dies or chips is a common solution for 3D‐integration today.

===== [p188-189] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.2 Origin of clamping forces (7块) =====
--[p188]-- K.2 Origin of clamping forces
The electrostatic force is one of the four fundamental physical forces<sup>21</sup>, along with gravitation, strong interaction and weak interaction. Electrostatic interactions exist between electric charges. The electrostatic forces were discovered by Coulomb (1736‐1806) and captured by the following formula:
$$
--[p188]-- { \frac { 1 } { 4 \pi \varepsilon _ { 0 } } } = 8 . 9 8 7 5 5 \cdot 1 0 ^ { 9 } \left[ { \frac { N m ^ { 2 } } { C ^ { 2 } } } \right]
$$
The permittivity of free space is therefore
$$
\varepsilon _ { 0 } = 8 . 8 5 4 1 9 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 1 2 } \left[ \frac { C ^ { 2 } } { N } \right] 0 \mathrm { r } \left[ \frac { F } { m } \right]
$$
Coulombs law can be simplified to stat the force dependent on a charge and the electric field the charge is subjected to.
--[p188]-- As stated in the formula below, the electric field created by a charge is dependent of the distance to this charge, this is also illustrated in Fig. 9‐95.
<sup>21</sup> In physics, fundamental interactions (sometimes called fundamental forces) are the ways that the simplest particles in the universe interact with one other. An interaction is fundamental when it cannot be described in terms of other interactions.
$$
\overrightarrow { F _ { a b } } = q _ { a } \cdot \overrightarrow { E _ { b } }
--[p189]-- $$
$$
\overrightarrow { E _ { b } } = \frac { 1 } { 4 \pi \varepsilon _ { 0 } } \cdot \frac { q _ { b } } { r ^ { 2 } } \cdot \frac { r _ { b a } } { r }
$$
Electrostatic forces are proportional to the area of the electrodes. Therefore, the electrostatic force us often expressed in terms of pressure instead of force, since this is independent of area. This electrostatic pressure, in the case of two parallel plates $\left( \overrightarrow { d _ { a b } } / d = 1 \right)$ (Fig.
--[p188]-- \overrightarrow { F _ { a b } } = \frac { 1 } { 4 \pi \varepsilon _ { 0 } } \cdot \frac { q _ { a } q _ { b } } { r ^ { 2 } } \cdot \frac { \overrightarrow { r _ { b a } } } { r }
$$
Where ε is the electric permittivity and $\overrightarrow { r _ { a b } }$ is the distance between the two charges $\mathsf { q } _ { \mathsf { a } }$ and ${ \mathfrak { q } } _ { \mathfrak { b } } .$ The constant in Coulomb’s law was determined by Coulomb himself in vacuum.
$$

===== [p37-37] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 3.2.2.4 Conclusions (2块) =====
--[p37]-- 3.2.2.4 Conclusions
Evaluating the accuracy, throughput and vibrations analysis, it is concluded that the current machine design is not suitable for the new target.
 The throughput is much too dependent on gantry speeds, the limits are discussed in section 3.3
 The large error propagation and buildup make the current alignment concept unlikely to reach the required levels
--[p37]--  The vibration analysis showed that absolute and relative motions of critical components are in the order of microns and that vibrations resulting from reaction forces even reach to tens of microns

===== [p55-55] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 5.1.2 Assembly sequence (2块) =====
--[p55]-- 5.1.2 Assembly sequence
The assembly sequence is pretty straight forward. A die is picked up from the source wafer and mounted on the flexible carrier. Next the die is transported by this carrier to the placement location, during which additional processes like cleaning and inspection can be performed. At the target location the die is placed on the target wafer and released from the flexible carrier. Since the die is placed in the same orientation it is picked up in (see Fig.
--[p55]-- 5‐1), it does not need to be flipped. Therefore this process step can be omitted. If it is required to flip the die it is preferred to do this at a location where it does not prolong the cycle time, e.g. during pickup from wafer.

--[p104]-- The values given take into account the observation that the vibration requirements of many items depend upon the detail size of the process.
--[p103]-- B.6.5 Vibrations
Machine accuracy is affected by vibrations. Some of the vibrations in a machine are caused by the machine itself (e.g. reaction forces and fluctuating bearing stiffness). Other vibrations are caused by the environment the machine stands in. With increasing accuracy, the significance of floor vibrations increases. To assess the effect of floor vibrations while the actual environment is unknown, standard specifications can be used for estimations.
--[p103 [表]]-- One such criterion is stated in Fig. 9‐9 and Table 9‐3. The VC‐B standard was chosen as the indicated detail size is closest to the expected size of the features (TSVs) that can be used for alignment.

===== [p59-59] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 5.3.3 Conclusions (3块) =====
--[p59]-- Studying the error tree it was also found that vibrations are the most important error source when trying to reach submicron accuracies. The reason for this is their presence in almost all branches of the tree, from calibrations to marker measurements and actual placement. In order to reach the required accuracy it was found that the vibrations due to reaction forces should be less than about 0.5 [μm] 3σ.
The actual requirement of course varies with the other items contributing to placement errors.
--[p59]-- 5.3.3 Conclusions
Currently the co‐location measurement system is attached to a XY positioning system, making it viable to vibrations. Analyzing the error tree of the newly developed system it was found that with the current optical system an accuracy of around 2 [μm] is possible in vibrations are not taken into account. This is a good starting point to make the final accuracy increase to 1 [μm].
--[p59]-- The stationary location of the alignment system in the new PnP concept makes it possible to use a bit heavier optics, possibly increasing robustness and accuracy. Further analysis of the error tree showed that with an increase in camera resolution (to >2.3M pixels) and with lens distortions below 0.1%, an accuracy of 1 [μm] 3σ is viable in vibrations if are not taken into account.

===== [p209-209] Appendix L Experimental investigation of electrostatic tape > L.2.1.1 Workings (3块) =====
--[p209]-- Sample is pulled off and maximum hold is used to record the pulling force
The sample is attached to the pulling mechanism by a compliant spring $( \mathsf { C } { \approx } 1 { \cdot } 1 0 ^ { - 3 } \ \mathsf { N } / \mathsf { m m } )$ . By retracting the actuator, a force builds up in the spring. This force is transmitted to the sample. When the upward force on the sample is greater than the downward force supplied by the clamp, the sample will come loose.
--[p209]-- L.2.1.1 Workings
The workings of the setup are best explained by the steps carried out to perform a measurement.
1. The clamp is attached to the scale (load cell) and the scale is zeroed. Now the mass of the clamp and its attachment to the scale are excluded from the measurements
2. The sample is placed on the clamp and the (scale now shows compression due to mass of sample)
3. Electric potential is supplied to the clamp
4.
--[p209]-- This process is registered by the scale. During force build up in the spring, the scale will be pulled up a bit as well. When the sample comes loose from the clamp, the scale will return to its original position.

--[p158]-- CONFIDENTIAL
--[p202]-- There is one side note when comparing the clamping forces. The bipolar and multipolar clamp always have some spacing between the oppositely charged electrodes (in the plane of the clamped object). So in case of a bipolar/multipolar clamp, it is not possible to use the full area under the clamped object for clamping. Therefore the holding force (not pressure) is slightly lower. Again, this is because of the slightly smaller electrode area (order of few percent).
--[p152]-- CONFIDENTIAL
--[p202]-- \begin{array} { l } { { V _ { a i r 1 } = n \cdot V ^ { + } } } \\ { { V _ { a i r 2 } = n \cdot V ^ { - } } } \\ { { | V ^ { + } | = | V ^ { - } | } } \end{array} \} V _ { a i r 1 } ^ { 2 } = V _ { a i r 2 } ^ { 2 }
$$
The electric field in the air gap near the negative electrode is equal but opposite to the electric field near the positive electrode. Since the electric field for the monopolar and bipolar clamp are equal in magnitude, the obtained clamping force is similar.

--[p202]-- The difference between the two clamping methods is that for the monopolar clamp the total potential over the wires connected to the electrodes is V, while for the bipolar and multipolar clamp this is 2∙V. This brings us to the potential source of the original misunderstanding. --[p210]-- L.2.3 Parasitic electrostatic charge (OPTIONAL)
During cyclic sample attach and release charge can build up on the clamp and/or sample surface, thereby increasing the holding force in an uncontrolled way. To get an idea of the magnitude of this effect an electrostatic volt meter<sup>22</sup> can be used. One should realize that the buildup of electrostatic charge is very much dependent on the materials used.

===== [p60-60] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 5.4.1.2 φ­Z actuator (2块) =====
--[p60]-- 5.4.1.2 φ­Z actuator
Several different actuators were discussed in app. I.2.3. The actuator that fitted the requirements best was the INtegrated Electro‐Mechanical Actuator (INEMA). This parallel linear/rotary actuator was developed in a research project collaboration of Assembléon, Prodirve, Technobis, Tecnotion and TNO Science and Industry [18]. The design consists of a rotor with two sets of permanent magnets, one for the translation and one for the rotation.
--[p60 [表]]-- The stator consists of two sets of coils, again one for the translation and one for rotation. Specifications aimed at are stated in Table 5‐2.

===== [p32-32] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 3.1.1 Introduction (2块) =====
--[p32]-- 3.1.1 Introduction
The market for pick and place equipment and other back‐end semiconductor equipment has, unlike the market for front‐end equipment, many different players. Some companies only develop proprietary machines, others sell‐on these machines and there are also companies whose core business is the development and manufacture of back‐end equipment. This makes surveying the market problematic since much of the information is not publicly available.
--[p32]-- It is especially difficult to obtain information on equipment which is developed for in‐house use exclusively. Therefore, only the commercially available machines will be discussed.

===== [p6-7] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > Abstract (17块) =====
--[p6]-- To continue performance increase and cost reduction in the future innovative solutions are needed. A promising field is 3‐Dimensional Integrated Circuit technology (3D‐IC). This technology focuses on the stacking of dies. This reduces package size and distances between different chips, thereby increasing performance and reducing packaging. Current cost effective solutions to realize 3D‐IC do not exist.
--[p6]-- The assembly (actual stacking) of 3D‐IC is one of the major contributions to the total cost these 3D‐IC devices.  Part of the TNO 3D‐IC initiative “BlueBird” focuses on developing a pick and place machine to assemble these stacks. The pick and place project consists of two major parts. First, for the stacks of chips to function, the electrical contacts between stacks in the form of Through Silicon Vias (TSVs) need to be aligned with position accuracies down to 1  μm.
--[p6]-- Looking at current state‐of‐the‐art pick and place (PnP) assembly equipment it was found that commercially available equipment lacks the combination of speed and accuracy required. Current equipment does fit either the speed or accuracy requirement but not both. This illustrates the trade‐off between speed and accuracy.
--[p7]-- The two most promising were using an adhesive tape with reusable and thermally switchable glue based on Diels‐Alder switching chemistry. The other concept was a low voltage electrostatic clamp. Since the Diels‐Alder chemistry based adhesive is currently still being researched and therefore not yet sure to meet the requirements, the electrostatic clamp concept was investigated further.
--[p6]-- Abstract
Technology has become increasingly important in today’s world. Especially the electronics industry which has in recent decades through the continued development of ever smaller and more powerful products  rapidly taken an important role in our everyday lives and will continue to do so in the future. Currently most electronic components contain surface mount devices. With contemporary miniaturization and performance trends the packaging of these devices is becoming the limiting factor.
--[p6]-- This concept decoupled the pick and place locations in time as well as in physical location and allowed additional parallel processes to be performed between pick and place. The highest throughput was achievable by reducing the transport distance from pick to place location to a minimum. In this concept the source and target locations were places directly opposite to each other, allowing the components to be propelled from source to target within a minimal time frame.
--[p7]-- For the machine parts where both accuracy and throughput were critical, commercially available parts were proposed. With these parts it proved feasible that the accuracy and throughput target would be met. The single system that was not commercially available was the actual flexible carrier for the die transport.
By investigating possible clamping mechanisms to temporarily bond a die to the flexible carrier it was found that several mechanisms were plausible.
--[p6]-- This accuracy subject and corresponding metrology is researched in a parallel project to the current one. The current project focuses on developing a new assembly concept that is capable of placing five individual chips per second at minimal increase of machine cost. This increase in throughput is required to make 3D‐IC a cost effective solution.
--[p6]-- It was also found that it is likely that the reaction forces caused by the increased operating speeds make it nearly impossible to achieve the required accuracy. Ten PnP concepts for die to wafer bonding were developed based on performing parallel actions on dies. By either processing multiple dies or performing multiple tasks simultaneously on a single die, the expected cycle time was decreased and throughput increased.
--[p7]-- To assess the possibility of using electrostatics (based on the attractive/repelling force between charges) as a clamping mechanism for bare dies, a low cost experimental test setup was devised.  The results of the tests showed a good correspondence to the estimations resulting from a mathematical model. These results make it likely that it is possible to realize the missing piece from the flexible carrier concept, namely the carrier itself.
--[p7]-- Another challenge is the actual design of the flexible carrier and the required actuators (transport system). This transport system design is currently the subject of a new graduation project. It is also recommended to investigate the risk of electrostatic clamp induced electrical damage to the die with TSVs in an early stage of follow up research. This is one of the main remaining issues in making the electrostatic carrier successful.
--[p6]-- To find the limits of current machine designs, motion profile analysis was performed and conclusions considering achievable cycle times were made based on the required velocity, acceleration and jerk. Vibration measurements were performed on a commercially available PnP machine to assess the effect of increased operation speeds on achievable accuracy. It was found that increasing operating speeds would require a huge improvement in actuator performance.
--[p6]-- By comparing these concepts pair wise in a trade‐off process three processes were identified that had the best potential in achieving required throughput and accuracy. These three concepts were the following:  A rotating turret located between the pick and place locations that created reduced transport distance and allowed simultaneous picking and placing. Another was the flexible carrier concept in which transport of dies to the placement nozzle was provided by an intermediate carrier.
--[p7]-- The overlay measurement is provided by a co‐location vision system developed in the accuracy part of the BlueBird project. The measurement of die to target location is performed by a single vision system, allowing a very short measurement loop, thereby enhancing accuracy as compared to commercially available systems.
The possibility to realize the new concept with existing components was investigated.
--[p7]-- After studying these three concepts a bit further a final concept choice was made by comparing the advantages and disadvantages. The flexible carrier concept had an advantage in placement accuracy (due to lower reaction forces) and in the possibility of parallel processes and was therefore the mechanism of choice. Next, the potential of the flexible carrier concept was further researched. The workings of this concept are as follows:
--[p7]-- Evaluating the expected accuracy and throughput for the newly developed innovative machine concept, it is likely that the requirements of placing five dies per second at a position accuracy of 1 μm can be met. This enables the future cost effective assembly of 3D‐IC.
Future research is required in order to successfully realize the machine concept. One challenge is found in adapting the alignment system currently designed for 3 μm placement accuracy to the required 1 μm.
--[p7]-- The bare die is released from the dicing tape and is placed on a flexible carrier. This flexible carrier provides the transport of the die from the pick to the place location and allows additional processes, like cleaning and inspection to be performed close to the placement location, providing an opportunity to increase yield. At the placement location the die is released from the carrier and is placed on the target location on an XY wafer stage by a phi‐z actuator to create stacks of dies.

--[p35]-- 3.2.2.2 Accuracy
The accuracy of the Datacon machine was assessed by setting up a static error tree. This error tree showed the build‐up and propagation of errors during the different calibration routines and the actual pick and place cycle. The error tree and its explanation are elaborated on in app. D.3 and app. D.4. It was decided to build a static error tree instead of performing a dynamic analysis for three reasons.
--[p35]--  Measurement systems and actuators were inaccessible, to inject noise or another signal into the machine would require alterations to the machine wiring. This was found to be too risky and time consuming
 The errors contributing most to the total placement error were mostly related to camera errors, which operate at fixed shutter speeds and are not frequency dependent
--[p36]-- With the increased jerk, accelerations and velocity required to fulfill the throughput requirement, the vibrations contribution will only increase, thereby deteriorating accuracy further. To make the newly developed alignment system work for the new requirements (1 [μm] placement accuracy and 5 dies/sec. throughput), the reaction forces or their effect should be reduced.
--[p35 [表]]-- calibration routines and actual placement, of the error tree, thereby propagating and amplifying their effect on the deterioration of accuracy. The second column from the right gives an estimation of the total contribution to the placement error. This estimation was made by setting the targeted parameter to zero contribution in the error tree, and looking at the difference in final placement accuracy.

===== [p123-123] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.4.2.5 Theta accuracy (3块) =====
--[p123]-- D.4.2.5 Theta accuracy
The nozzle can rotate about its vertical axis. This provides the theta rotation capability of the system. This theta rotation is not only used in the actual positioning of the die, but also in some calibration routines. These routines depend on an exact theta motion. The $\tt X , \tt Y$ error caused by an error in the theta rotation is discussed here.
--[p123]-- 9‐32 illustrates how the system interprets the measurement, causing an XY error between the global (G0) co‐ordinate system and the local (nozzle) co‐ordinate system.
--[p123]-- Some actions, for example the ‘substrate camera – bond head calibration’, depend on an exact $\boldsymbol { 1 8 0 ^ { \circ } }$ theta rotation of the nozzle [34]. If the rotation is not exactly 180°, this causes an XY measurement error because the actual marker position is not where it is expected. Fig. 9‐31 illustrates the actual nozzle rotation with theta error, and Fig.

===== [p222-222] Appendix L Experimental investigation of electrostatic tape > L.8.1 Conclusions (3块) =====
--[p222]-- Short circuiting the electrodes allowed the charge on both to neutralize and the clamped object was released. Thereby it was indeed confirmed that a capacitive clamp was made since the JR forces are caused by charges at the interface of clamp and sample, and these charges are more difficult to remove.
--[p222]-- This value was used to compute the expected holding pressure as function of voltage. The maximum measured force as function of voltage fitted the expected values well. Therefore it is concluded that an electrostatic clamp was successfully engineered.
To verify that the clamp operated by capacitive clamp theory and not by Johnson Rahbek forces (see appendix Appendix K), it was verified that the clamping force was lost immediately as the charge on the electrodes was removed.
--[p222]-- L.8.1 Conclusions
A capacitive clamp was manufactured from simple components. Aluminum electrodes were deposited on a Kapton foil insulator film with good dielectric properties. By capacitive measurements with a 200 [nm] flat surface as counter electrode, it was found that the flatness of the clamp in terms of average air gap was about 3.4 [μm]. With the (less flat) sample electrode attached the average air gap was $4 . 6 \pm 0 . 3$ [μm].

===== [p18-19] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > 1.1 Basic layout of an electronic device (8块) =====
--[p19]-- According to ${ \mathsf { I P C } } ^ { \prime } { \mathsf { S } } ^ { \mathrm { 4 } }$ standard J‐STD‐012, “Implementation of Flip Chip and Chip Scale Technology”, a CSP shall have an area no more than 1.2x the area of the original die size and is direct surface mountable [2]. Since the current state‐ofthe‐art package is already so small, there is only limited gain in reducing the size of this conventional package.
To increase density of electronics on a certain surface area, another approach should be used.
--[p19]-- In the past decades the electronics industry has succeeded in creating ever smaller packages and thereby greater functionality density, this resulted in the ever smaller electronic products [1]. Today we are at the level of chip scale packages (CSP), where the package is almost no larger than the chip itself.
--[p19]-- From the illustrations above it is visible that the die size and structures on it are much smaller than the space occupied by the package and the size of the ${ \sf P C B ^ { \prime } S }$ . Therefore, the most functional part of the electronic device is very small compared to the total footprint of the complete system. Indicating there is still a lot to gain in this area.
--[p18]-- <sup>1</sup> Die: a small piece of silicon wafer, bounded by adjacent scribe lines in the horizontal and vertical directions, that contains the complete device being manufactured. Also called chip and microchip. [55]
2 One nanometer is equal to 1000 micrometer (or micron). One micron is about 1/25<sup>th</sup> of the diameter of a human hair. One meter consists of one million micrometers
(1 meter = 10<sup>6</sup> micrometer or [μm] = 10<sup>9</sup> nanometer or[nm])
--[p18]-- 1.1 Basic layout of an electronic device
The functional part of the electronic device is the chip or $^ { \prime } { \mathsf { d i e } } ^ { 1 , }$ . This contains the integrated circuit (IC) which by means of transporting and storing small charges (electrons) provides the actual functionality. This functionality can for instance be electronic logic (like processors) or memory.
--[p18]-- One can imagine an IC as an electronic highway system on which electrons travel at great speed to fulfill the requests of the user. The structures on a die are very delicate and have typical dimensions ranging down to 32 nm (nanometer<sup>2</sup>), which is approximately a thousand times smaller than the thickness of a human hair.
--[p19]-- The semiconductor industry has shown their ability to keep pace with Moore’s law<sup>3</sup>, and provide us with an ever increasing diversity of functions on a die. Traditionally single dies are placed inside a package which provides protection and the ability to connect it to a substrate. After packaging, the package is placed on a PCB to create a connection with other chips and other electronic components. This forms a functional device that can be found in any electronic apparatus. Fig.
--[p19]-- 1‐5 gives a schematic overview of the basic layout of such a device. The outer (visible) layer (Fig. 1‐5.C) of the device is the package. This package protects the internal functional component (Fig. 1‐5.A) from external influences, and also protects the environment from any harmful matter inside this functional component if present. The electrical connection between the outside world and the functional component is provided by some conductive material (Fig. 1‐5.B) inside the package.

===== [p108-108] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.1.1 General layout of pick and place unit (2块) =====
--[p108]-- From the point where the die is initially picked up from the wafer by the flip units the pick and place part of the
machine has basically two simultaneous, parallel pick and place systems. To simplify the analysis only the left side of the pick and place system is described.
--[p108]-- D.1.1 General layout of pick and place unit
There are seven main components of the pick and place unit. The function of each is described below.
 Wafer table
 Flip unit
 Gantry (X,Y)
 PhiZ actuator
 Alignment system
 Dip unit
 Placement location
The machine has two gantries work in parallel. Both have a single nozzle attached to the gantry system. Effectively this means that the machine has about double the throughput of a system with a single gantry.

===== [p118-119] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.4.1.3 Pick and place process (3块) =====
--[p118]-- It should be noted that in ‘fast mode’, the up looking camera measurement is not performed. The accuracy defining steps are:
 Measure die location on nozzle with SC and ULC
 Pre positioning (measurement of substrate marker with SC)
 Adjustment stroke
 Placement
D.4.1.3.1 Measure die location on nozzle with SC and ULC
As illustrated in Fig. 9‐27, the ULC looks at the die position and orientation.
--[p118]-- D.4.1.3 Pick and place process
During the pick and place process a die is picked from a wafer by one of the flip units. The die is presented to the bond head, which in turn dips the die in some flux or glue. Next, the accuracy is obtained by measuring the die location and orientation on the nozzle at the ULC. Subsequently the die is moved above the substrate, where the SC measures the placement marker. A small adjustment step is made, after which the nozzle moves down and the die is placed.
--[p119]-- Combined with the SC measuring the location of the ULC, the relative position and orientation of the die position and orientation with respect to the SC is calculated.
Bond head  substrate camera offset
 Marker accuracies
 SC calibration
 ULC calibration
 ULC  ULC marker distance
 Vibrations (external, roller guide, reaction forces)

===== [p158-158] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > H.3 Alignment concepts for flexible carrier concept (1块) =====
--[p158]-- H.3 Alignment concepts for flexible carrier concept
This section briefly discusses some alignment concepts for the flexible carrier concept. The boundary conditions for the alignment system are
PhiZ nozzle attached to machine frame
 XY wafer stage provides horizontal alignment
 TSV locations can be used as fiducials or fiducials are available where needed

===== [p159-159] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > H.3.2 Measure die relative to carrier (2块) =====
--[p159]-- Any shift of the die relative to the carrier, results in additional placement error. This is a similar principle as is currently used in the Datacon machine. The large error propagation over different required calibration routines and the large off‐line placement stroke make this concept less suitable for high accuracy.
--[p159]-- H.3.2 Measure die relative to carrier
First measure the die location on the carrier relative to markers on the carrier. This step can be performed before the carrier reaches the placement location. Next measure the carrier relative to the placement substrate and use a phi‐z nozzle for placement with an optional high repeatability XY motion.
The drawback of this system is that two different measurement steps have to take place.

===== [p86-86] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 8.1.1 System analysis of pick and place equipment (3块) =====
--[p86]-- An analysis on a current state‐of‐the‐art pick and place machine (the Datacon 8800 FC Quantum) was performed to assess the possibility of modifying this machine to achieve both high throughput and high accuracy. Motion profile analysis showed that it is not realistic to use a single gantry system to pick and place 5 dies/sec for a travel distance of 300 [mm].
--[p86]-- 8.1.1 System analysis of pick and place equipment
A study of current pick and place machine illustrated the current trade‐off between accuracy and throughput, making it difficult to achieve both high throughput and high accuracy at the same time. Generally Surface Mount Technology (SMT) equipment has high throughput and low accuracy and Micro Systems Technology (MST) equipment has high accuracy but low throughput. The die bonder pick and place machines have both average throughput and accuracy.
--[p86]-- This was mainly due to the large difference between current stateof‐the‐art accelerations of stages and the required acceleration to achieve low enough cycle time. An error tree analysis and complementary vibration measurements on the Datacon machine showed that if the required accelerations are achieved, it is unlikely that the accuracy requirement can be met with the current design.

===== [p152-152] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > G.1 Trade­off process (3块) =====
--[p152]-- G.1 Trade­off process
To determine which preliminary concept has the most potential to evolve into a successful design, the different concepts were compared pair wise on several criteria. An Analytical Hierarchy Process Trade‐off Tool was used for this purpose. This tool provided a systematic way of evaluating the concepts.
The APH Trade‐off tool is an excel tool based on the Analytical Hierarchy Process as developed by Dr. Thomas Saaty.
--[p152]-- The final score of each concept is based on a multiplication of the score of each concept on each criteria, multiplied with the weighting factor of this criteria.
--[p152]-- For the construction of this tool, use has been made of the CASETS AHP trade‐off tool from which Ir. R.J. Hamann (Delft University of Technology) has constructed a stand‐alone version [42]. In this tool a hierarchy of trade‐off criteria is entered in a tree structure. Each branch of the tree gets weighting factors based on pair wise comparison of the importance of trade‐off criteria. The different concepts are then compared pair wise considering the different criteria.

--[p219]-- L.5.4.2 Corrected data
To remove the results affected by residual charges from the measurements, the air gap corresponding to the measured force was calculated. If the air gap was more than 0.4 [μm] smaller than the expected 4.6 ± 0.3 [μm], the measurement was discarded. This data correction was not applied to the measurements with cleaned samples, since these did not have any residual charges.
--[p219]-- It was also verified that the data points representing the maximum values found for the corrected measurements (blue line in Fig. 7‐24), were during the measurements not directly followed by a measurement that contained residual charge. This way it was assured that the obtained maximum values were not affected by residual charges at the interface. As with the previous data, the ones where the clamp and sample were cleaned before the measurement are summarized separately (pink line in Fig.

===== [p180-180] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.2.4 Choice of clamping mechanism (8块) =====
--[p180]-- Furthermore, the usage of electrostatic (wafer) clamps is already done in front‐end machines. The risk of damaging the die is however still a risk, and potential failure modes should be investigated. Like the vacuum tape concept with continuous supply, the electrostatic tape also requires some continuous supply.
--[p180]-- Despite a well working on/off behavior, the tape release mechanism causes potential contamination and the adhesive is not reusable. The adhesive currently being developed by TNO based on Diels Alder chemistry shows potential but is not nearly a finished product [21].
The vacuum concept was subject to some considerable limitations. F
First, if a vacuum pump is used it proved difficult to get the suction to the die.
--[p180]-- However, an adhesive that meets these specifications does not exist today. Therefore the adhesive tape concept is not the best option for now. An interesting on/off switch principle was developed by Philips Applied Technologies, where a laser aimed at the backside of the dicing tape caused the adhesive to heat up, producing exploding gas bubbles that propelled the die from the tape [17].
--[p180]-- This is however less a problem to accomplish with electricity than for air flow since for instance, simple dragging contacts could be used, that are already present in many electro motors. A more elegant solution in preferred though. The good potential of the electrostatic clamp as well as the innovative concept make the electrostatic tape very interesting for further study. The fundamentals of electrostatic clamps are further elaborated on in Appendix K.
--[p180]-- In the trend of energy consumption reduction concerns have been raised about the usage of vacuum pumps [48]. Furthermore, measurements to the Datacon machine revealed large vibration caused by the vacuum pump, deteriorating the placement accuracy of the die on the substrate.
Second, the tape with internal cavities concept looks viable, but concerns exist about the conflicting needs for both low and high stiffness in the tape. Potential leakage is the main problem with the concept.
--[p180]-- J.2.4 Choice of clamping mechanism
Three potential clamping mechanisms were identified, some of which had considerable downsides to them when investigating the concept a bit further. The adhesive concept does show potential, but current technology is a limiting factor. If an adhesive is developed that creates no residual particles larger than a few micron, has an on/off behavior under an external influence (for example temperature) and is reusable, the adhesive tape concept has potential.
--[p180]-- The sliding tape concept is viable to friction and wear and the limitations in routing flexibility of the tape leaves nothing to be desired. For the tape with internal channels it was difficult to come up with a good solution to connect the stationary source to the moving tape, besides two failure modes were identified where the internal channels were narrowed. Using a vacuum pump for continuous suction supply is also expensive because of the large power draw of these pumps.
--[p180]-- If the seal is not perfect, the under pressure is lost because there is no continuous airflow supplying suction.
The electrostatic concept shows the most potential. The main reason for this is the switchable clamping force. Potential damage to the electronic circuits is unlikely due to the low voltage required to obtain the required holding pressure. The required 5 [V] over the air gap is close to the voltages normally used to operate the ICs on the dies.

===== [p201-201] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.7 Monopolar and multipolar design (3块) =====
--[p201]-- The bipolar clamp has two oppositely charged plates and requires no electrical connection to the object to be clamped. An extended design of the bipolar clamp is the multipolar clamp that has more than two oppositely charges plates, but works by the same principle as the bipolar clamp.
For bipolar and multipolar designs a positive voltage is applied to one plate and a negative to the other.
--[p201]-- While for the monopolar clamp the object is actually charged, the bipolar clamp works via charge redistribution inside the charged object and therefore, the net charge on the clamped object remains zero. For the bipolar clamp, the object can be equipped with a grounding wire to remove any charge that is build up at the interface for some reason.
--[p201]-- K.7 Monopolar and multipolar design
In electrostatic clamp design a clear distinction can be made between two different designs. The two ways to create an electrostatic clamp are shown in Fig. 6‐4. The upper is a monopolar clamp and the lower is a bipolar or multipolar clamp. The monopolar clamp has an electrical connection to the charged plate as well as to the object to be clamped.

===== [p73-74] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.1.2 Risk of electrical damage (7块) =====
--[p74]-- With current technology trends continuing to shrink the gate oxide thickness in order to achieve faster switching speeds, gate oxide breakdown in transistors is becoming a more and more prevalent failure mechanism. Though some circuits can continue normal operation even after a few gate oxide breakdowns, this is clearly not a general case. Breakdown in a critical transistor will cause failure, damaging the circuit and therefore the whole IC [26].
--[p74]-- An oxide layer can break down instantaneously at 8‐11 [MV] per centimeter of thickness, or 0.8 ‐ 1.1 [V] per nanometer of thickness [27]. As reported in [28], 1.0 [nm] oxide layers are being researched, which is close to the limit of 0.7 [nm] as proposed in [29]. A typical foundry<sup>12</sup> uses 17 [nm] gate oxide thickness for 0.6 [μm] CMOS processes [30].
--[p74]-- To evaluate the risk of voltage build up over the dielectric for higher voltages, three different scenarios are distinguished for clamping a bare die with electrostatics.
--[p74]-- This is even more true because the transistors do not operate so close to the limits of the oxide layer, therefore it can be assumed that the breakdown voltages are somewhat larger.
As other features of the device are scaled down, so must oxide thickness. Oxides become more vulnerable to the voltages fed into the device as they get thinner. The thinnest oxide layers today are already less than 5 [nm] thick.
--[p73]-- 7.1.2 Risk of electrical damage
To assess the risk of damaging the ICs on the die, first a basic understanding of the build‐up of ICs is required. The most widely used technology for manufacturing ICs is the complementary metal‐oxide‐semiconductor or CMOS technology. This technology is used to construct anything from microprocessors, microcontrollers, memory and other digital logic circuits. Key components of the ICs are the Metal‐oxide‐semiconductor field effect transistors (MOSFET, Fig.
--[p73]-- 7‐4) and capacitors (Fig. 7‐5). Both components are constructed from conductive and dielectric parts, where two conductive patches are separated by a dielectric, usually silicon dioxide.
The critical failure mode for transistors and capacitors is similar to that of the electrostatic clamp itself, that is dielectric breakdown. If a large enough potential is applied over the dielectric, it will be damaged or destroyed.
--[p74]-- Because of this, it should be avoided that a large potential builds up over the oxide layer. For illustrative purposes, Fig. 7‐6 shows a metal gate of a transistor that is destroyed by forces generated by the oxide breakdown.
Transistors in current chips switch with voltages of 1.8 to 5 [V]. So, if the voltage over the oxide layer due to the clamping mechanism does not exceed this, damage to the IC is avoidedo.

===== [p138-139] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > F.4 Multi nozzle (3块) =====
--[p138]-- The average cycle time for a single die is about 350 ‐ 450 ms. This excludes the additional time needed for measurements and the actual placement of the die. Current systems using multiple nozzles in line or in an 2D array are limited to about 8 nozzles (for example 2 rows of 4 actuators).

$$
--[p138]-- F.4 Multi nozzle
This process is very similar to the single nozzle approach. The difference is that instead of a single die, multiple dies are collected at the source location and transported to the placement location. This parallel transport reduces the amount of times the transport distance needs to be covered. Since this transport step from source to target position is very time consuming, the cycle time can be reduced drastically.

--[p72]-- 7.1.1 Introduction to the electrostatic clamp
An electrostatic clamp uses the electrostatic forces between opposing charges to create an attractive force between two surfaces. Because the force is proportional to the area of the electrodes, the term electrostatic pressure is often used instead of force. For a purely capacitive electrostatic clamp containing an air gap and an insulating layer between the electrodes (Fig. 7‐1), the electrostatic pressure is given by the formula below.

--[p37]-- 3.3.1 Motion profile analysis
The most obvious way to increase throughput is to use higher velocities, accelerations and jerk. This section discusses the effect on cycle time when these parameters are increased for a die to wafer PnP process. Both the source and target wafer are 300 [mm] wafers, resulting in an average distance to be covered  in the order of 300 [mm] (Fig. 3‐7). ===== [p101-101] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > B.3 Stack (2块) =====
--[p101]-- B.3 Stack
Increasing the number of dies in a stack has implications on the alignment system used. For example, in the back‐end semiconductor industry vision systems are generally used for fiducial detection. These camera’s have a limited focus depth, meaning the camera lens, aperture or position has to be altered for changing stack heights. Also, dies are expected to have a non‐uniform thickness, causing stacks on a single wafer to have slightly different heights.
--[p101 [表]]-- The number of layers in a stack, and therefore stack thickness is expected to change over the coming years. Evaluating the stack height for the values stated in the BlueBird roadmap (Table 1‐1), the following stack heights are expected (Table 9‐2).

===== [p86-86] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 8.1.2 Conceptual design (5块) =====
--[p86]-- It was also found beneficial for achieving to locate the φZ DOFs at the die and the XY DOFs at the wafer. The main limitation of the flexible carrier concept is that it is relatively difficult to change the product to be assembled. It is possible a different carrier is needed for a component with a different shape.  This limitation has however limited impact in case of 3D‐IC since full wafers with many identical components are processed.
--[p86]-- Moreover, bare dies are mostly very similar since only thickness and outer dimensions change per wafer. The flexible carrier also enables processes to be performed on the dies while being transported from the pick to the place location. This is beneficial since it is likely that a cleaning step between pick and place is required.
Many commercially available components (e.g. stages) already meet the requirements for the new PnP concept.
--[p86]-- 8.1.2 Conceptual design
Ten different concepts were proposed for transporting dies from the source wafer to the target wafer for die to wafer assembly. A trade‐off showed separating the large transport motion from the high accuracy placement process is beneficial for both throughput and accuracy. The flexible carrier concept had the highest potential to meet the requirements. This resulted from the short travel distances required at the placement location.
--[p86]-- In the pursuit of high accuracy it becomes increasingly interesting to look at air bearing stages because of their low friction. For die to wafer assembly many less critical components from the current Datacon machine (e.g. wafer handling) can be used. It might be possible to adapt the BlueHawk alignment principle for 1 [μm] (3σ) placement accuracies, the vibrations caused by reaction forces should then be reduced to at most 0.5 [μm] (3σ).
--[p86]-- If a suitable alignment mechanism and placement unit are developed, it is highly likely that the newly developed concept reaches both the required accuracy and throughput requirements.

===== [p175-176] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.2.3.2.1 Monopolar and multipolar clamps (13块) =====
--[p175]-- To get an idea of the severity of this problem some initial calculations were performed on the required charge and electrical potential. Current IC’s typically work at core potentials between 1.2 [V] and 5 [V].
First the required holding force in terms of pressure is calculated. Using pressure as opposed to force, this is useful because the holding force scales with electrode area.
--[p175]-- \begin{array} { l } { { F _ { a c c } = m \cdot a \big [ N \big ] } } \\ { { \ } } \\ { { F _ { h o l d } = \displaystyle \frac { F _ { a c c } } { \mu } = \displaystyle \frac { m \cdot a } { \mu } \big [ N \big ] } } \\ { { \ } } \\ { { P _ { h o l d } = \displaystyle \frac { F _ { h o l d } } { A } = \displaystyle \frac { m \cdot a } { \mu \cdot A } = \displaystyle \frac { l \cdot w \cdot h \cdot \rho \cdot a } { \mu \cdot l \cdot w } = \displaystyle \frac { h \cdot \rho \cdot a } { \mu } \Big [ N \cdot m ^ { - 2 } \Big ] } } \\ { { \ } } \\ { { P _ { h o l d \_ m a x } = \displaystyle \frac { 1 5 0 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 6 } \cdot 2 3 3 0 \cdot 2 5 } { 0 . 1 } \approx 1 \cdot 1 0 ^ { 2 } \Big [ N \cdot m ^ { - 2 } \Big ] } } \end{array}
$$

--[p175]-- The calculations below illustrate that required holding pressure is also independent of die area, making comparing pressure a sensible choice. The required holding pressure does however depend on die thickness, density, maximal acceleration and friction coefficient between die and carrier.
$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { a = 2 5 \Big [ m \cdot s ^ { - 2 } \Big ] } \\ & { \rho _ { s i } = 2 3 3 0 \Big [ k g \cdot m ^ { - 3 } \Big ] } \\ & { \mu _ { f r i c t i o n \_ m i n } = 0 .
--[p175]-- J.2.3.2.1 Monopolar and multipolar clamps
In electrostatic clamp design a clear distinction can be made between two different designs. The two ways to create an electrostatic clamp are shown in Fig. 6‐4. The upper is a monopolar clamp and the lower is a bipolar or multipolar clamp. The monopolar clamp has an electrical connection to the charged plate as well as to the object to be clamped.
--[p175]-- The term is usually used in the electronics and other industries to describe momentary unwanted currents that may cause damage to electronic equipment.
Therefore, the static permittivity of air is assumed equal to that of vacuum. Also, only a single electrode is shown in this network. Therefore the figure represents a monopolar clamp. The resulting holding force is however the same as for the bipolar clamp, independent of electrode configuration. This is illustrated in Fig. 9‐78.
--[p175]-- The electrical properties of the electrostatic connection is assessed by assuming the electrical network shown in Fig. 9‐79. For calculating the capacitance of the air layer it is assumed the relative permittivity of air is 1.
<sup>18</sup> Electrostatic discharge (ESD) is the sudden and momentary electric current that flows between two objects at different electrical potentials caused by direct contact or induced by an electrostatic field.
--[p175]-- The bipolar clamp has two oppositely charged plates and requires no electrical connection to the object to be clamped. An extended design of the bipolar clamp is the multipolar clamp that has more than two oppositely charges plates, but works by the same principle as the bipolar clamp.
The most likely setup is the lower one, the bipolar clamp, since it is not desirable have the need for an electrical connection to the die itself.
--[p176]-- For simplified force calculations one can however neglect the insulator layer and assuming the full voltage is available over the air layer. The electrostatic pressure is now estimated with the following formula.
$$
\begin{array} { l } { { f = - { \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \varepsilon _ { \scriptscriptstyle 0 } \cdot \varepsilon _ { r } \cdot { \displaystyle \frac { V ^ { 2 } } { d ^ { 2 } } } } } \\ { { F = f \cdot A } } \end{array}
$$
--[p176]-- Where $\varepsilon _ { 0 }$ and  ε are the vacuum permittivity and the relative permittivity of air respectively. The vacuum permittivity is $8 , 8 5 4 8 1 0 ^ { - 1 2 } \ [ \mathsf { F / m } ] ,$ , and the relative permittivity of air is again assumed 1, as stated before. V is the voltage over the air gap, in [V], and d is the air gap height, in [m]. f is the resulting electrostatic pressure in [Pa].
--[p176]-- This graph also illustrates why the bipolar clamp doesn’t require an electrical connection. Charge inside the clamped object is redistributed here, while for the monopolar clamp the object itself is charged.
J.2.3.2.2 Electrostatic force estimation
As described in app. K.3, the electrostatic force is dependent on several parameters that describe the effect of the (monopolar) voltage divider effect (Fig. 9‐79) of the air layer and isulation layer between the electrodes and the clamped object.
--[p175]-- This is preferred because the electrical connection takes time to establish and disconnect. Also, the die is charged with the risk of residual charges remaining on the die after removal. The use of electrostatics in combination with sensitive electronic circuits is not trivial. Usually measures are taken to avoid charging electronic circuits, one reason for this is the risk of electrostatic discharge $\left( \mathsf { E S D } \right) ^ { 1 8 }$ .
--[p176]-- For the required pressure of 100 [Pa] and an assumed air gap of 1 μm, the required voltage is about 5 [V]. Electrical damage to the electronic circuits is therefore unlikely as the required clamping voltage is close to the voltages normally used to operate the ICs on the dies.

===== [p67-67] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 6.3.3 Suction (2块) =====
--[p67]-- Several configurations of a vacuum clamp combined with a flexible carrier are discussed in app. J.2.3.3 and an overview is given in Fig. 6‐6 to Fig. 6‐8. The advantages and disadvantages of using suction for the clamping mechanism are stated below.
--[p67]-- 6.3.3 Suction
In many, if not most, current PnP machines, the clamping mechanism is realized by a vacuum nozzle. Therefore, it makes sense to investigate the possibility of using suction to hold dies on the flexible carrier. The way the suction principle works is by creating an under pressure between the die and the carrier. This results in the atmospheric pressure pushing the carrier and die together.

===== [p122-123] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.4.2.4.2 Roller guides induced vibrations (5块) =====
--[p123]-- u \frac { \partial } { \partial t } = \frac { \partial a / \partial t } { { \omega } _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } } = \frac { j } { { \omega } _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } }
$$
$$
u = \frac { j } { \omega _ { 0 } ^ { 3 } }
$$
if positive jerk is followed by negative jerk
without much delay, the effects can be added (worst case)
$$
u = 2 \frac { j } { \omega _ { 0 } ^ { 3 } }
$$
--[p122]-- Due to the reaction forces and suspension stiffness as well as internal stiffness of the frame, the frame will vibrate. These frame vibrations in turn cause assembly parts attached to the frame to vibrate. The limited stiffness between the frame and parts attached to it causes these to move relative to another. If this effect occurs on an assembly which requires high precision, some of its accuracy is lost.
--[p123]-- The second effect is related to measurement of a certain reference dimension instead of the actual dimension where the accuracy is required. Due to limited stiffness of the connection between the reference point and the critical part, the critical dimension to be controlled is susceptible to errors due to vibrations.
In both cases the resulting vibrations are similar. The initial error for a cubic motion profile is related to its jerk.
--[p122]-- D.4.2.4.2 Roller guides induced vibrations
The roller guides induced vibrations are caused by the fact that the compression of the rollers, due to load/preload, changes. The number of rollers carrying the load changes with the configuration of the system. The significant factors are; the stiffness of the guide, the number of balls and the load.
The first effect occurs when the reaction forces from an actuator are directed into the frame, as is often the case.
--[p123]-- To obtain the relation between jerk and vibrations a differential equation had to be solved. This equation is not reported here. Instead a simplified reasoning is shown below. Again this was not the actual used derivation.
$$
\Delta x = u
$$
$$
k \cdot u = F = m \cdot a
$$
$$
u = a \cdot \frac { m } { k } = \frac { a } { \omega _ { \mathrm { 0 } } ^ { 2 } }
$$
$$

--[p82]-- 7.3.3.1 Uncertainties and data corrections
Both the force sensor used for the force measurements, as well as the capacity measurements have some uncertainties. The used force sensor had a resolution of 0.5 [mN] and non‐linearity of 0.1% over 2.5 [N]. For the 1 [cm<sup>2</sup>] electrode, this scales to ±25 [Pa]. The capacitive measurements showed a spread of 5%, which translated in ±0.3 [μm] for the air gap height.

===== [p101-101] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > B.6.1 Stand­off distance (2块) =====
--[p101]-- The TSV bond pads extend a bit outside the die itself. These bumps are basically spacers between the dies in a stack (Fig. 9‐7). The bump height is related to the diameter of the TSVs. For 5 μm TSVs, 3 μm bumps are used.
It is difficult to get a good estimate of the surface quality that will be obtained for the dies. It is however known that the bumps of the stacked dies should be in contact in the final product. Therefore, the bumps should be larger than the surface roughness of the die.
Resulting in the bump height being the most important parameter in determining the stand‐off distance of the dies.
--[p101]-- B.6.1 Stand­off distance
The vertical distance between two dies in a stack is related to two subjects.
1. The dies themselves are not completely flat. Thick wafers show both warp and bow. Thin wafers have much lower in plane stiffness (scales with thickness to the third). When placed on a flat surface the thickness non‐uniformity is dominant for surface quality. Over a full wafer thickness non‐uniformity of about 6 μm has been reported [32].
2.

===== [p72-72] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.1 General explanation of electrostatic clamp (2块) =====
--[p72]-- Damaging the electrical circuits on the die is unacceptable and is therefore discussed after the required holding pressure and voltage is calculated based on estimated disturbance forces. Electrical time constants were also obtained using simulations.
--[p72]-- 7.1 General explanation of electrostatic clamp
This section discusses some parameters to be taken into account when designing an electrostatic clamp or flexible carrier. First, a very brief introduction to the electrostatic force is given. A more elaborate explanation is included in Appendix K. As concluded in section 1.1, there are several points that have priority in designing an electrostatic clamp for the flexible carrier concept.

===== [p157-157] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > H.2 Vision systems (3块) =====
--[p157]-- CCD sensors typically have a higher signal to noise ratio and sensitivity but are also more expensive than CMOS. Due to the lower cost, CMOS becoming more popular.
There are many parameters affecting the accuracy of a camera. The main ones considering the image processors are:
 Pixel size
o Larger  better signal to noise ratio
o Smaller  more pixels (higher accuracy)
 Chip size
o Larger  larger pixels / more pixels
--[p157]-- Some illustrations of fiducials are shown below.
Two different commonly used image processors are Charge Coupled Devices (CCD) and Complementary Metal‐Oxide Semiconductor (CMOS). Both sensors capture an image in a grid of pixels. The main difference between both sensors is the way the sensor are read out. CCD sensors have to be read out one line at the time, while with CMOS sensors the pixels are controlled individually.
--[p157]-- H.2 Vision systems
The dicing process to separate the individual ICs is a rather course and violent process in which silicon is removed between the dies. The actual position of the dicing street with respect to the IC on the die is not controlled with sufficient accuracy for pick and place alignment reference. This makes the outside of the die useless for accurate alignment of the ICs. For this reason vision is usually applied to measure markers on the die, called fiducials.

===== [p29-29] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > 2.3 Throughput (1块) =====
--[p29]-- 2.3 Throughput
A cost of ownership analysis has shown that the throughput of the pick and place machine has to be increased to 5 dies/sec or 18k units per hour (UPH) for the die‐to‐wafer stacking process to be economically viable [8]. A numerical example is discussed in app. B.2.

===== [p102-102] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > B.6.2 Bonding (3块) =====
--[p102]-- No consensus exists in industry about the location of the bumps on the TSVs. This can therefore either be on the top, bottom or even on both sides of the die [33].
--[p102]-- B.6.2 Bonding
Bonding of the dies can basically be performed in two ways. One can choose to make the full bond with the PnP machine (e.g ultrasonic bonding). Or a temporary (mechanical) bond is made in the PnP machine and the final (electric) bond is created with e.g. collective soldering. The first option is very interesting since it reduces the steps in the total process flow.
--[p102]-- The technology to do this in a way the current requirements on speed and accuracy are met does however not exist today. Therefore, the choice was made to use the two step process. The implications for the PnP tool are the following.
 A temporary bond will be made in the PnP machine.
 The current preference is to use BCB adhesive
 The required bond time is 1020 [ms]
 in which a maximal force of up to 15 [N] has to be applied.

===== [p87-88] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 8.2.2 Flexible carrier related (3块) =====
--[p87]-- 8.2.2 Flexible carrier related
 Rigid parts inside the flexible carrier could be used to provide mechanical support to the die when it is released from the dicing tape, thereby reducing the chance of mechanical damage
Only the electrostatic clamp carrier was investigated. If the Diels‐Alder switching chemistry works, this could be a very interesting solution as well
 The design of the electrostatic clamp should be further investigated
--[p87]-- o Required holding force should be assessed by multi‐physics analysis to take into account the peeling effect
o The electrical damage through TSVs risk should be further researched, discussed with IDMs and tested
o Adhesive strength of the temporary die bond should be investigated to quantify the maximum release force from the carrier
o The attraction of dust by the electrostatic clamp should be quantified and its effect on contamination control determined.
--[p88]-- Using a conductive base material could reduce stray fields o In future measurements a mechanism should be used that avoids the peeling effect
o The use of electrolyte instead of two metal electrodes with a voltage supply should be investigated. Initial discussions lead to believe that enough electrostatic force can be obtained this way. This application of electrolyte was not investigated in the current thesis but is certainly interesting for future studies
o Initial discussions with industry indicated there might be interest in using an electrostatic clamp instead of a vacuum nozzle

===== [p49-49] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 4.4.2 Flexible carrier concept (2块) =====
--[p49]-- When the die reaches the placement location, a short stroke z‐φ actuator places the die on the target wafer. The XY positioning is performed by the wafer stage. Optionally, the rotation DOF can be located at the wafer stage as well. The position measurement required for final alignment can be implemented in two ways.
● Relative measurement of target and die at the placement location (preferred)
 First measure die relative to flexible carrier (arbitrary location along flexible carrier length).
At the placement location only the nozzle relative to the wafer is measured.
--[p49]-- 4.4.2 Flexible carrier concept
Dies are picked from the source wafer either by the flexible carrier itself or by an optional flip unit. Along the length of the flexible carrier, additional process steps can be performed on the dies which are cleared from the pick and place locations. An additional flexibly sized buffer can be added by routing the flexible carrier between adjustable rollers.

===== [p170-170] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.1.4 Damage to die (2块) =====
--[p170]-- J.1.4 Damage to die
Needless to say, the transport system should not damage the die to such extent that functionality or reliability is affected. Different types of damage are distinguished.
 Contamination
o Foreign particles should not enter process layer
o Particles larger than bump size or bump pitch could affect electrical contact
 Chemical alteration of die
o For example oxidation or implantation of particles
 Electrical damage
o The ICs and TSVs should not be altered
--[p170]-- o Die should not be left with harmful electric charge (quantification requires further study)
 Mechanical damage
o Chipping of silicon
o Cracking of die or die perimeter
o Abrasion of silicon, process layer, bumps
o Mechanical damage causes particles  contamination

===== [p206-207] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.8.4.2 Difficult release (3块) =====
--[p207]-- When at t = 500, the voltage sources are grounded (or short circuited), the capacitive clamp discharges quickly (capacitive clamp RC time). However, charge still remains at the interface, keeping the holding force in place. This charge will be removed on the long JR time constant again. Therefore, for fast declamping, it is preferred not to build up JR forces. This can be accomplished by using an insulator with a high resistivity.
--[p206 [表]]-- K.8.4.2 Difficult release
If the voltage divider on JR time causes a larger potential over the air gap (due to reduced insulator resistivity), the potential over the air gap continues to rise after the buildup of the capacitive clamp forces. This is illustrated with the time simulation below, using the parameters in Table 9‐27
Table 9‐27 Model parameters for JR force buildup evaluation
Insulator (1) Air gap (1) | Die (1) | Insulator 2 | Air gap 2 | Die (2)
C [pF] | 60 | 430 | 60 | 430
--[p206]-- R [Ω] | $2 \cdot { 1 0 } ^ { 1 2 }$ | $2 . 6 \cdot { 1 0 } ^ { 1 2 }$ | $\boldsymbol { 1 8 \cdot 1 0 ^ { 3 } }$ | $2 \cdot { 1 0 } ^ { 1 2 }$ | $2 . 6 \cdot { 1 0 } ^ { 1 2 }$ | $\boldsymbol { 1 8 \cdot 1 0 ^ { 3 } }$
In the first second, the capacitive clamp is charged. In the time interval to 500 [s] charge is build up at the interface of both capacitors due to leakage currents over the insulator, increasing clamping pressure.

===== [p83-84] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.3.4 Other findings (6块) =====
--[p84]-- This clamp unfortunately failed at a low voltage by dielectric breakdown, before any clamping force was obtained. The failure was detected by the voltage sources indicating a short circuit. After removal of the sample electrode it was obvious that a dielectric breakdown occurred in the $\mathsf { S i O } _ { 2 }$ layer of both (positive and negative) electrodes of the clamp. The sample electrode was also damaged and showed embedded Kapton particles when examined under a microscope (Fig. 9‐145).
--[p83]-- 7.3.4 Other findings
During the measurements it was found that several effects interfered with obtaining good consistent measurement data. Three effects were identified that reduced the measured force and two were found to have amplified it. These effects are further discussed in app. L.8.2.
Deteriorating clamping force due to
Dust between clamp and sample electrode (reduced clamping force)
 Peeling off of sample instead of instant release (measurement error)
--[p84]--  A 1 inch wafer, thickness 200 [μm], was picked up with the clamp (Fig. 7‐25). Shaking the clamp by hand or deliberately trying to blow it off did not release the wafer. When the clamp was short circuited, discharging the electrodes, the wafer was released.
$\mathsf { A } 5 \times 5$ [mm] die, thickness 700 [μm], was also picked up with the clamp (Fig. 7‐25).
An electrostatic clamp with a $\mathsf { S i O } _ { 2 }$ layer as insulator was also manufactured.
--[p84]-- Elastic deformation of the Kapton insulator layer (reduced effective clamping force
Higher clamping force due to
 Residual charges at insulator  sample electrode interface (unwanted increase, charge difficult to remove)
 Capillary forces resulting from remaining alcohol which was used for cleaning
--[p84]-- For most measurements, the sample was only placed on the clamp for a very short time (a few seconds), With the calculated time constant in the micro second range (see Table 7‐2), it can be safely assumed that the full clamping force was obtained in this time. To find out if the Johnson Rahbek effect was visible in a reasonable time frame, the sample was left clamped for a few minutes.
--[p84]-- If the JR effect was present on a short time scale it was expected the sample would release by itself due to the reduced clamping force (explained in app. K.8.4). This effect was not observed, but no quantification of the JR effect was made since this would result in single experiments of over 10 minutes.
For the force measurements above, a sample electrode was used instead of actual dies or wafers. To verify the clamp would work with dies and wafers as well, some simple tests were performed.

===== [p34-34] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 3.2 Analysis of the Datacon 8800 FC Quantum (4块) =====
--[p34]-- 3.2 Analysis of the Datacon 8800 FC Quantum
The BlueBird project involves the stacking of bare dies (or chips). The machines required for die bonding (which is most similar to die stacking) are found in the market of the die bonders. In this market, the Datacon 8800 FC Quantum (hereafter referred to as the Datacon machine) has a very good throughput to accuracy ratio compared to the other players in this market.
--[p34]-- For this reason amongst others, TNO has obtained such a machine and is currently developing a new alignment system for it. The current performance is about 3600 to 8000 components per hour at an accuracy of 10 to 25 [μm] (3σ) respectively. The aim of the new alignment system is to realize an accuracy of 3 μm at acceptable loss of throughput (lower target marker in Fig. 3‐2 and Fig. 3‐3).
--[p34]-- The aim of the current project is to develop a new PnP concept based the Datacon machine, with much increased throughput and accuracy (upper target marker in Fig. 3‐2 and Fig. 3‐3). By keeping as much of the original system as possible, the chances of this concept being adopted by Datacon are increased. This should however be a goal, but not a restriction to the development.
--[p34]-- To successfully accomplish this, a good understanding is required of the layout, workings and limitations of the Datacon machine. The workings and general layout of this flip chip pick and place machine as well as the functional diagram are discussed in Appendix D. A short summary is included below, followed by the performance analysis.

===== [p189-189] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.3 Capacitive clamp (3块) =====
--[p189]-- K.3 Capacitive clamp
The properties of a capacitive clamp have to be determined for designing such a clamp. As discussed in the previous section, the clamping force is dependent on the height of the air gap, the voltage drop over it and the dielectric material between the capacitor plates. An electrostatic clamp always has an insulating layer over one of the electrodes.
--[p189]-- Charge now also moves between the bottom plate of the top capacitor and the top plate of the bottom capacitor so the net charge of each capacitor is zero.
CONFIDENTIAL
--[p189]-- This insulating layer also has dielectric properties and therefore introduces a second capacitor in series with the capacitor over the air gap (Fig. 9‐97). The way the capacitive clamp works is that upon applying the voltage, the top plate of the top capacitor and the bottom plate of the bottom capacitor receive charge from the voltage source.

===== [p190-190] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.3.1 Electrical network (8块) =====
--[p190]-- a i r } } { C _ { a i r } + C _ { i n s } } ( \begin{array} { l } { { V _ { a n s } \approx V _ { s } \cdot \displaystyle \frac { \varepsilon _ { 0 } } { C _ { 0 } } } } \\   V _ { i n s } \approx V _ { s } \cdot \displaystyle \frac  \displaystyle \frac \end{array} \end{array}
$$
The equivalent total capacitance of the series connection is
$$
\begin{array}{c} \left.
--[p190]-- } \varepsilon _ { r } + d _ { i n s } } { A \varepsilon _ { 0 } \varepsilon _ { r } }  \\ { \displaystyle C _ { i n s } = \varepsilon _ { 0 } \varepsilon _ { r } \frac { A } { d _ { i n s } } } \\ { \displaystyle C _ { e q } = \frac { { \mathcal A } \varepsilon _ { 0 } \varepsilon _ { r } } { d _ { a i r } \varepsilon _ { r } + d _ { i n s } } } \end{array}
$$
For each additional layer between the two electrodes, an additional capacitor and resistance have to be added in series with the current circuit.
