--[p190]-- K.3.1 Electrical network
This second capacitor also causes the voltage difference over the air gap to be different from the supplied voltage since the circuit is acting as a voltage divider. It should be noted that there is always some leakage current through the resistance. Therefore a resistance is added parallel to the capacitor(Fig. 9‐97). The voltage over the air gap can be calculated by Ohm’s law. First the impedance for each layer is determined.
--[p190]-- For these calculations, the wire resistance is neglected because this is much less than the resistance of the capacitors. Therefore the voltage drop in the wire is neglectable.
$$
Z _ { a i r } = R _ { a i r } + { \frac { 1 } { i \omega C _ { a i r } } }
$$
$$
Z _ { i n s } = R _ { i n s } + \frac { 1 } { i \omega C _ { i n s } }
$$
Next, the voltage over each layer is calculated.
If the resistance of a layer is very high, the capacitive term is dominant in the impedance equation, even for low frequencies.
$$
--[p190]-- \begin{array} { r l r } & { } & { V _ { a i r } = V _ { s } \cdot \cfrac { Z _ { a i r } } { Z _ { a i r } + Z _ { i n s } } \approx V _ { s } \cdot \cfrac { C _ { i n s } } { C _ { a i r } + C _ { i n s } } } \\ & { } & { V _ { i n s } = V _ { s } \cdot \cfrac { Z _ { i n s } } { Z _ { a i r } + Z _ { i n s } } \approx V _ { s } \cdot \cfrac { C _ { a i r } } { C _ { a i r } + C _ { i n s } } } \end{array}
$$
Expanding this equation in the parameters of the capacity yields
$$

===== [p143-143] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > F.6 Turret supply (1块) =====
--[p143]-- F.6 Turret supply
The placement turret (lower one) is mounted to a rigid frame and places the dies on the target wafer, which is moved on a wafer stage. The placement turret is continuously supplied by two or more supply turrets which unload one or more source wafers and double as flip unit. Either the source wafer or supply turret can be moved during unloading of the wafer.

===== [p76-76] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.1.3 Forces acting on die (5块) =====
--[p76]-- This assumption is supported by the good correspondence between the expected clamping values and performed clamping force measurements, as discussed in chapter 7.3. The figures below illustrate the forces that are taken into account for determining the required holding force.
Since the forces discussed here are dependent on the die size and thickness, the benchmark 25 [mm<sup>2</sup>] square die is assumed with a maximum thickness of 200 microns.
--[p76]-- 7.1.3 Forces acting on die
During displacement of the clamped die, several forces are acting on the die which should be resisted by the clamping force. The most important forces are those due to accelerations including gravity, and forces due to interactions with the air (Fig. 7‐11). Another important force occurs when the clamp base material is not stiff enough and deforms when going around bends (Fig. 7‐12).
--[p76 [表]]-- <sup>13</sup> A redistribution layer consists of conductive material that is used to relocate the IC bond pads over the chip surface, this is a commonly used technique for flip chip [58]
Table 7‐1 Indication of required clamping pressure

--[p76]-- The table below gives an indication of the required clamping pressure based on rough estimates. The calculations are included in app. J.3. Note that these are rough estimates based on simple calculations. To get more accurate estimates it is advised to perform multi physics modeling. This way the interactions between mechanics, aerodynamics and electrostatics can be estimated.
--[p76]-- This in turn results in bending of the die or release of the die if the clamping force is not sufficient. Besides the forces mentioned above there are also other forces acting on the die like chemical adhesion to the carrier and van der Waals forces. These forces, amongst others, are neglected here since the magnitude of these forces are expected to be lower.

===== [p110-110] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.1.1.5 Dip unit (1块) =====
--[p110]-- D.1.1.5 Dip unit
The dip unit contains some flux which is applied to the die before placement. The gantry system moves the chip above the dip unit and dips the underside of the die in flux by moving the Phi Z actuator down. Again, the location of the dip unit and a close up are illustrated below.
The dip unit consists of a surface where the die is dipped (red encircled in Fig. 9‐23) and a mechanism to level the dip area after dipping to prepare for the next die.

===== [p109-109] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.1.1.2 Flip units (2块) =====
--[p109]-- D.1.1.2 Flip units
The flip units are located just above the wafer table. These units pick the die from the diced wafer, flip it over, and supply it to the gantry system’s pick and place nozzle. The location of the two flip units is illustrated in Fig. 9‐16, and a close up view is shown in Fig. 9‐17. From the flip unit on there are basically two identical paralle systems for processing the dies and placing them on the substrates.
--[p109]-- Each flip unit contains a nozzle and actuators to rotate around the Y‐axis (flip action) and to move the unit in the vertical direction towards the wafer for the pickup action.

===== [p154-154] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > G.2.12 Complexity (mechatronic) (1块) =====
--[p154]-- G.2.12 Complexity (mechatronic)
The mechatronic complexity of a process concerns both the engineering effort needed to realize a certain concept as well as the risk that a system malfunctions. Also, increased complexity often leads to a more expensive system. The complexity is therefore best minimized.

===== [p117-117] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.4.1.2.3 Bond head – Substrate camera offset (2块) =====
--[p117]-- D.4.1.2.3 Bond head – Substrate camera offset
The bond head – substrate camera offset is a very critical dimension. This dimension is used in other calibration routines and also during the actual pick and place actions. Whenever some sort of marker is measured by the SC, followed by a nozzle (attached to bond head) operation. This dimension is used to determine the offline stroke (also see app. D.2).
This calibration routine measures the offset of bond head and substrate camera.
--[p117]-- A special die (BMC die) is placed on a special calibration unit, after which its location is measured by the SC.
Measured values: Offset of substrate camera center and bond head center
Error sources contributing to the error are:
 BMC die accuracy
 Calibration unit accuracy
 G0 matching
 SC calibration
 Theta rotation accuracy (horizontal die rotation)
 Vibrations (external, roller guide, reaction forces)

--[p215]-- L.5.2 Expected clamping force
The force measurements are performed with the lapped counter electrode. From Table 9‐32 it follows that the air gap is about 4.6 [μm], with an uncertainty of 0.3 [μm].
Fig. 9‐135 shows the expected clamping pressure for this air gap.

--[p125]-- D.4.2.8 Gantry bending
The two gantry systems are capable of moving in X,Y and Z. A small Rz movement is also possible by using both X motors, but this is not further discussed here since it is not used by Datacon. The X linear motors are connected to the fixed world, the Y system is stacked on the X system, and the Z system is placed on the Y system. This is illustrated in the picture below.
During movement of the system, there is elastic deformation of the aluminum Y beam.
First of all, the beam can bend during an acceleration in X‐direction.

===== [p177-178] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.2.3.3.1 Vacuum force estimation (11块) =====
--[p177]-- 1 } \\ & { \frac { d _ { d i c } } { d _ { u x } } \approx 1 } \\ & { f = \frac { h _ { d i c } \cdot \rho _ { d i c } \cdot a } { \mu } \cdot \frac { d _ { d i e } \left[ N \cdot m ^ { - 2 } \right] } { d _ { u x } } } \end{array}

J.2.3.3.2 Continuous supply – porous carrier sliding on vacuum chucks
A porous carrier can be slid over a vacuum chuck to supply the die with a vacuum and motion (Fig. 6‐6).
--[p178]-- It is also an expensive one because of the cost of the vacuum supply system and chucks. The carrier – chuck friction may cause the it to wear out fast, which results in down‐time of the machine.  This is certainly not desirable. Therefore this concept will not be investigated further.
--[p178]-- The largest concern with this concept is the friction between the carrier and the vacuum chucks. This friction makes it difficult to move the carrier. The minimal force pushing the carrier on the vacuum chucks, and therefore causing friction, is the holding force of the dies. However, the carrier itself has an internal pressure drop as well. This causes an additional force which pushes the it on the vacuum chucks.
--[p177]-- $\mathsf { A } _ { \mathsf { v a c } }$ is the area on the die the vacuum is supplied on. When a clamping method is used which makes use of some kind of porous carrier, the $\mathsf { A } _ { \mathsf { v a c } } / \mathsf { A } _ { \mathsf { d i e } }$ ratio is close to one. When using some kind of nozzle, this ratio is less than one, thereby increasing the required pressure.
--[p177]-- For the concept using a porous carrier with continuous vacuum supplied, the required pressure is less. The calculations below show that the required holding pressure is independent of die area. It does however depend on die thickness, density, maximal acceleration and friction coefficient between die and carrier. Since these parameters are the same as for the electrostatic clamp, the required holding pressure is again 100 [Pa].
$$
--[p178]-- The friction coefficient between the carrier and the chucks makes the it difficult to move and it is likely that the flexible carrier will be stretched if it is pulled on. To meet the condition of a switchable force the pressure at the placement location can be reduced or even removed during die release from the carrier.
Supported by the explanation above, it seems that this concept could be possible, but it isn’t a nice solution because of friction and wasted energy.
--[p177]-- J.2.3.3.1 Vacuum force estimation
The required pressure for a vacuum clamp is calculated as follows
$$
--[p177]-- 5 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 5 } [ m ^ { 2 } ] } \\ { { \scriptstyle A _ { v a c } } = 2 \pi \cdot r ^ { 2 } = 2 \pi \cdot 0 . 0 0 1 ^ { 2 } \approx 6 . 3 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 6 } [ m ^ { 2 } ] } \end{array}  \scriptstyle M = \pmb { H _ { d e } } \cdot \pmb { \rho _ { d e } } \cdot \pmb { a }  & { { \scriptstyle A _ { d e } } _ { \cdot n e } \approx 4 \cdot 1 0 ^ { 2 } [ N \cdot m ^ { - 2 } ] = 4 [ m b a r ] }
$$
--[p177]-- \begin{array} { l } { { F _ { a c } = m \cdot a \big [ N \big ] } } \\ { { F _ { b a l } = \frac { F _ { a c } } { \mu } = \frac { m \cdot a } { \mu } = \frac { A _ { d e } \cdot h _ { d e } \cdot \rho _ { d e } \cdot a } { \mu } \big [ N \big ] } } \\ { { F _ { v a c } = f \cdot A _ { v a c } \big [ N \big ] } } \\ { { F _ { b a l } = F _ { v a c } \Rightarrow \frac { A _ { d e } \cdot h _ { d e } \cdot \rho _ { d e } \cdot a } { \mu } = f \cdot A _ { v a c } \Rightarrow f = \frac { A _ { d e } \cdot h _ { d e } \cdot \rho _ { d e } \cdot a } { A _ { v a c } \cdot \mu } \Big [ N \cdot m ^ { - 2 } \Big ] } } \\ { { f = \frac { h _ { d e } \cdot \rho _ { d e } \cdot a } { \mu } \cdot \frac { A _ { d e } } { A _ { s a c } } \Big [ N \cdot m ^ { - 2 } \Big ] } } \end{array}
$$

--[p36]-- Some important results considering deterioration of the placement accuracy are stated below.
 Significant vibrations were found resulting from vacuum pump (100 Hz) and housing fans (200 Hz) o See red circles in Fig. 35
 Reaction forces from the wafer stage caused highest machine vibrations levels
 Reaction forces from the gantry system cause vibrations in the bond head attached to this gantry
 Reaction forces from one gantry cause vibrations in the bond head attached to the other gantry

--[p36]-- 3.2.2.3 Measurements
To verify the errors due to random vibrations and reaction forces and to get a better understanding of machine operation and machine limits, vibration measurements were performed. The full report of the measurements performed by the current author are included in Appendix M. Since the findings resulting from these measurements were an important basis for the newly developed concept it is advised to read the introduction and conclusions of this measurement report.

 Force dependent on surface quality of die and clamp
 High power consumption of vacuum systems
 Some concepts sensitive to contamination

The investigated concepts for combining a vacuum clamp with the flexible carrier showed some severe limitations. For the concepts with a vacuum pump (Fig. 66, Fig.
--[p67]-- 6‐7) it proved difficult to come up with a good solution to connect the stationary vacuum pump to the moving flexible carrier, either by sliding over vacuum chucks or by using internal tubing integrated in the flexible carrier. The concept with internal cavities inside the flexible carrier (Fig. 6‐8) has the limitation that any small leak in the seal, reduces holding force over time, finally releasing the die in an uncontrolled manner.
--[p66]-- It should be noted that the simple scenario of an electrostatic clamp with only an air gap is discussed here. Actual clamps also have a dielectric insulator layer between both electrodes.
Electrostatic fields attract dust, as is known from many household applications (e.g. filter in vacuum cleaner), therefore this topic does require special attention when designing a clamp system.

Preliminary results look promising, but the research is still on‐going. Since commercially available alternatives do not suffice, there is currently no suitable alternative that makes the chemical adhesive concept viable.

===== [p174-174] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.2.3.1 Chemical adhesion (5块) =====
--[p174]-- J.2.3.1 Chemical adhesion
Tapes with a sticky adhesive material on them are used in front‐end as well as back‐end semiconductor applications extensively. For example, wafers are placed on a dicing tape before they are diced. And most SMT pick and place machines get their components supplied on adhesive tape. Tapes designed to hold bare dies also exist. One supplier of these tapes is Surftape [46].
--[p174]-- Adhesives with some form of on/off switch are also being investigated. One adhesive which is very interesting because of its on/off behavior is the Diels‐Alder based glue, currently being developed by TNO [20] [21] [22]. This glue’s adhesive properties vary with temperature and exhibit a certain reversible on/off behavior over a specific temperature range (covalent bonds in adhesive change with temperature).
--[p174]-- Although this seems like a simple solution, there are however downsides to using an adhesive tape for a temporary die bond. Some are stated below.
 The particles of the adhesion layer of the tape which can remain on the die (leave residue)
 Very thin dies can be damaged during release from the sticky layer
 The time required to release the die from the tape depend heavily on the type of adhesive used, and most likely longer than for a mechanism with a switchable holding mechanism
--[p174]--  The release force is expected to be considerable
Cost is a concern since these tapes are usually used once and then disposed, therefore they are machine consumables
The most common problems with tapes are the contamination, the residual adhesive remaining on the grasped object after release. And the high release force (equal to grasp force) could damage the die during release (mechanical stresses, Fig. 9‐76).
Currently there are adhesives under development with less and less residual adhesive.
--[p174]-- The glue is also being designed so it leaves much less residue as compared to commercial alternatives and preliminary results on the thermal on/off behavior look promising. The adhesive is however not finished yet, and is therefore not a definitive solution for the current project.

===== [p136-136] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > F.1 Placement of DOF for precision alignment (3块) =====
--[p136]-- There are also solutions where the z,φ  DOF are controlled at the die and the $\mathsf { x } , \mathsf { y }$ at the target, for example in the Panasonic MSR. Often, the high throughput systems have all DOF controlled at the die, and the systems with higher accuracy have separated systems for xy and zφ alignment, with xy located at the substrate and zφ located at the die.
--[p136 [表]]-- Considering the two horizontal movements are similar, there are twelve possible configurations (3!\*2=12).
Die | Target | Die | Target | Die | Target
x,y,2,φ | None | $\times , 2 , \Phi$ | y | ${ } _ { z , \Phi }$ | x,y
None | x,y,z,φ | x,y | z,φ | X | y,z,φ
x,y,z | φ | X,Z | y,φ | Z | x,y,φ
x,y,φ | Z | x,φ | y,z | φ | x,y,z
Existing solutions often have all DOFs located at the die, for example using a gantry system as used in the Datacon 8800 FC Quantum and the Siemens Siplace X4i.
--[p136]-- F.1 Placement of DOF for precision alignment
For the die to wafer assembly process there are four degrees of freedom to be controlled by the pick and place machine during the final alignment of the die to the target. The tree translations in X,Y and Z and the rotation about the die axis parallel to the TSVs have to be controlled.
These DOF can be controlled either by the stage holding the target or the stage holding the die.

===== [p161-161] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > H.4 Conclusion (3块) =====
--[p161]-- H.4 Conclusion
The limited accuracy of the two step vision method makes it less suitable for the current application. The interferometer system can provide the required accuracy and probably even much better than is currently required. But the complexity and possibility of error propagation of this system are a potential problem. This makes the co‐location technique the method of choice for 3D‐IC stacking.
--[p161]-- Currently the co‐location measurement system is attached to a XY positioning system, making it sensitive to vibrations. Analyzing the error tree of the newly developed system it was found that with the current optical system an accuracy of around 2 [μm] is possible. This is a good starting point to make the final accuracy increase to 1 [μm]. The stationary location of the alignment system in the new PnP concept makes it possible to use a bit heavier optics, possibly increasing robustness.
--[p161]-- Further analysis of the error tree showed that with an increase in camera resolution (to >1M pixels) and lenses with less distortion, an accuracy of 1 [μm] 3σ is viable (further discussed in section 5.3.2).

--[p22]-- Vertical integration will provide an answer to four different market demands.
Form factor. All functionality in mobile devices like smart phones requires further integration of the semiconductor devices to fit in the housing. Vertical integration is already used in I‐Phones and in the new Nokia models where the camera and related logic chip are stacked.
Performance. Fast DRAM requires a combination of memory and logic.
--[p22]-- Stacking these chips results in better performance and higher production yield compared to integration in a single chip where production requirements conflict.
Memory capacity. Hard disk drives are replaced by flash memory because of lower power consumption and robustness. However this requires an extra integration technique on top of Moore’s law to store more bits in a few cubic millimeters.
--[p22]-- Vertical integration is here the technology of choice for leading companies like SD, Samsung and Micron.
Processor power consumption. A microprocessor with 3D integration will use up to 20% less silicon real‐estate and energy since shorter distances enable reduced signal amplification.

===== [p32-32] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > Fig. 3‐1 Vacuum nozzle holding component while it is placed on a printed circuit board (PCB) (2块) =====
--[p32]-- Fig. 3‐1 Vacuum nozzle holding component while it is placed on a printed circuit board (PCB)
Another back‐end application where pick and place machines are used is the bonding of bare dies to lead frames or even directly to PCBs, called flip chip (see section 1.2.1). These applications usually require greater accuracy than SMT and the components are much less robust, resulting in reduced throughput.
--[p32]-- Die bonders generally have medium throughput and accuracy as compared to SMT and MST (Micro Systems Technology). Typical players in the die bonder market are ASM, Besi and TDK. Highest typical accuracy and throughput reached by these machines are 7000 UPH and 10 [μm] (3σ) placement accuracy. Again, the high placement accuracy is not necessarily reached at the highest throughput and also varies with the type of component placed.

===== [p176-176] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.2.3.3 Vacuum (1块) =====
--[p176]-- J.2.3.3 Vacuum
The way the suction principle works is by creating an under pressure between the die and the tape. This results in the atmospheric pressure pushing the tape and die together. A vacuum clamp can be created by either creating a local vacuum inside the tape which holds the die or by using a porous tape which is continuously supplied with a vacuum. Continuous supply is can for example be realized either by channels inside the carrier or by sliding the carrier over vacuum chucks.

--[p0]-- Delft University of Technology

===== [p79-79] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.2.4 Base material (2块) =====
--[p79]-- 7.2.4 Base material
The base material can be either conductive or nonconductive. If a metal is chosen, the electrical field outside the die/wafer will be spread out, but over the bottom insulating layer. This has as benefit that there is less electric field to the environment which can attract dust. On the other hand, using a metal does require a good insulation layer to prevent leakage currents.
In addition, a metal base increases the risk of charge leaking from the clamp.
--[p79]-- Moreover, an insulating base material makes the test clamp a bit safer to handle. A readily available, easily manufacturable insulator material is glass. Therefore, a flat glass plate was used as base material for the test setup clamp.

===== [p21-21] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > 1.2.2 3D­integration with TSVs (7块) =====
--[p21]-- 1.2.2 3D­integration with TSVs
The high‐end industries (e.g. memory and processors) are searching for ever increased performance. As an analogy to wire bonds being replaced by flip chip assembly to connect a die to a package, a similar solution was proposed by IBM in 1969 for stacking dies without wire bonds. More, shorter and faster connections between dies can be realized with Through Silicon Vias (TSVs) in a shorter assembly time.
--[p21]-- Such 3‐Dimensional‐Integrated Circuits (3D‐ICs) are extremely attractive for overcoming the barriers in interconnect scaling, and offer an opportunity to continue the electronics industries performance trends for the next decade.
By integrating a very large System on a Chip (SoC) in multiple tiers, the average distance between system components is reduced, this in turn improves performance. Therefore, nowadays the focus is set on connecting dies directly together by stacking them.
--[p21]-- <sup>6</sup> The mission of the international consortium $\mathsf { E M C } ^ { 3 \mathsf { D } }$ is to rapidly develop a cost‐effective and manufacturable TSV (thru‐silicon‐via) for 3D chip stacking and micro‐electro‐mechanical systems (MEMS) integration. [32]
<sup>7</sup> The 3D‐IC Alliance is a consortium of integrated circuit designers, developers, and manufacturers.
--[p21]-- The process required to create a stack of dies with TSV interconnects is a heavily researched topic because of the many technical and economical challenges and opportunities related to it. There are a few consortia occupied with these topics (for example EMC3D<sup>6</sup> and 3D‐IC Alliance<sup>7</sup>). The TNO BlueBird initiative is one of them.
--[p21]-- Its objective is to promote standards for three‐dimensional integrated circuits (3D‐ICs) in order to accelerate their availability and acceptance. Industry‐wide standards would allow virtually any semiconductor vendor to implement 3D technology. [54]
--[p21]-- A TSV is an electrical contact through the silicon chip to make contact with the chip above or below it, in a package with vertical integration. Through these TSVs data is transported from one chip to another in the stack. So a TSV is the elevator in the silicon skyscraper (Fig. 1‐9).
Recent progress in the fabrication of TSVs has opened new avenues for high density area array interconnects, which can for example be used to connect stacked processors and memory chips.
--[p21]-- This way, multiple chips are packaged in a single package, creating higher functionality per surface area. This results in improved performance, lower power usage and smaller components [6]. It is estimated that in a few years from now, more than 10% of the chips will use some form of vertical integration [7].

--[p108]-- D.1 Workings of the Datacon 8800 FC Quantum flip chip bonder
The Datacon 8800 FC Quantum flip chip machine is a flip chip die bonder. This means that the bare die is flipped before it is bonded to the substrate. This is in the contrary to for example chip shooters to which the chips are generally supplied on tape and which do not flip the chip over before placement.
The overview of the different machine areas is shown in Fig.
--[p108]-- 9‐13, as well as the orientation of the right‐handed Cartesian coordinate system.

 Blue ‐ Pick and place
 Green ‐ Pumps, electronics and other supply systems
For this project only the pick and place part of the machine is of interest for now, because it determines the overall throughput speed. Therefore only this part is discussed here.

===== [p200-201] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.6.2 Dielectric breakdown (3块) =====
--[p200]-- K.6.2 Dielectric breakdown
A second limitation for the capacitive clamp is the maximum electric field over the insulator. For a capacitive clamp it is generally preferred to have a high dielectric constant, to have less parasitic capacitance of the insulator, and a high dielectric strength, to allow high voltages over the insulator. Both dielectric strength and dielectric constant are not fully independent of film thickness, but for typical film thickness it can be assumed as such.
--[p200 [表]]-- When dielectric breakdown occurs, the capacitor plates discharge over the dielectric. This causes local failures in the dielectric layer, but as long as both electrodes are still separated, the clamp is not fully destroyed. Based on the data in Table 9‐25, the Kapton film from DuPont shows best performance.
Table 9‐25 Dielectric properties of several films
Film | Dielectric strength [V/μm] | Dielectric constant | Dissipation factor
Kapton 100 HN (25 μm) | 303 | 3.4 | 0.0018
--[p201]-- Mylar 92A (23 μm) | 275 | 3.25 | 0.005
PET | 275 | 3.3 | 0.002
ETFE | 120 | 2.6 | 0.008
ECTFE | 107 | 2.56 | 0.005

===== [p35-35] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 3.2.2 Performance (1块) =====

===== [p52-52] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 4.5 Choice of concept (5块) =====
--[p52]-- 4.5 Choice of concept
Concepts similar to the turret concept are already available on the market and this concept has been analyzed for high placement accuracies in a previous pick and place study [13]. A conclusion from the trade‐off study performed in this work was that, for on wafer (e.g. die to wafer) assembly it is beneficial to have the XY alignment located at the wafer and Zφ located at the die. This is similar to what is proposed for the flexible carrier concept.
--[p52]-- This issue is addressed in Chapter 6 and Chapter 7. The overview of this concept and examples of commercially available hardware to realize this concept are presented in the next chapter.
--[p52]-- The current flexible carrier concept was not considered in the previous study. The wafer to wafer assembly has been topic of earlier research as well [17]. Here ‘laser die transfer’ was considered; vaporizing the dicing tape glue with a laser resulted in exploding gas bubbles, propelling the die away from the dicing tape.
--[p52]-- Primarily because it has the highest score in the trade‐off study. Second because the flexible carrier concept has, to the knowledge of the author, not been studied earlier for the current requirements. The current master thesis is a good opportunity to investigate the potential of the flexible carrier concept further. The critical part of the flexible carrier concept is the carrier itself, especially how to fix dies temporarily to the carrier without damaging or contaminating them.
--[p52]-- During the project it was found difficult, to control the ‘launching process’, initially resulting in very high launch speeds of 30 [m/s] with unsatisfactory placement accuracy. Further research reduced speeds to 1 [m/s] with satisfactory accuracy for the application (surface mount technology). Actual obtained accuracy is unknown, but typical SMT accuracy is lower than what is required for die stacking.
The current master thesis will focus on the flexible carrier concept.

===== [p157-157] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > H.1.1 Accuracy (1块) =====
--[p157]-- H.1.1 Accuracy
Early results indicated a 3 [μm] 3σ placement accuracy is feasible, as was expected form the error tree. The largest components in the error tree are related to calibration errors (1 [μm]), measurement of die and substrate markers (1.5 – 2 [μm]) and placement process inaccuracies due to vibrations (1 [μm]). Vibrations are of course also present during measurements and calibration. Therefore a large fraction of the obtained error is due to vibrations (> 2 [μm]).
Excluding all vibration contributions, the system has a potential accuracy of about 2 [μm] 3σ.

===== [p169-169] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.1.2.2 Bond­ and de­bond­time (2块) =====
--[p169]-- The de‐time required for debonding the die is directly affecting the placement cycle time, and is therefore more critical. It is estimated there is about 20 [ms] available to release the die from the carrier for a 200 [ms] total cycle time. This can however vary depending on when the die is released, being either before placement (use intermediate vacuum nozzle) or after placement (keep carrier attached during final placement).
--[p169]-- J.1.2.2 Bond­ and de­bond­time
If the die bonding to the flexible carrier and the actual placing cycle are decoupled in time, the average time available to place a die on the flexible carrier is equal to the total cycle time (200 [ms]). In this time span the carrier should be moved as well. The allowable bonding time is estimated at 100 [ms]. But reduced bonding time also results in reduced start‐up time. Therefore it is preferred to have even less bond time.

During my work I was supervised by Prof.ir. R.H. Munnig Schmidt of the TU Delft. The daily supervision at TNO Science and Industry was performed by Drs. T.C. van den Dool and ir. R.W.J. Knaapen.

--[p2]-- Preface
This thesis describes the work I have performed from June 2009 to March 2010 in order to graduate as Master of Science (MSc.) in Mechanical Engineering at Delft University of Technology (TU Delft) in the department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering, section Mechatronic System Design. The assignment I worked on was provided by program manager back‐end‐semiconductor equipment ir. A.A.A. Kastelijn at TNO Science and Industry in Delft.
--[p2]-- The work I have performed is part of the research project “BlueBird” which is a TNO initiative. The goal of this project is to develop enabling technologies for 3D‐IC, which is an innovative concept for assembling chips for future applications. The goal of my project is to find the limitations of current state‐of‐the‐art assembly equipment for electronic components, and to develop an innovative assembly concept for 3D‐IC.

===== [p194-194] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.3.2.3 Relative permittivity (dielectric constant) (6块) =====
--[p194]-- Because of the large dipole moment of water, it has a high relative permittivity of 80 (at 20°C). This means that for given capacitor dimensions, water can store 80 times more charge than vacuum. The increased capacitance is a result of the dipoles aligning with the electric field, thereby creating additional negative charge at the electrode – dielectric interface. Therefore, the electrode can store additional positive charge, hence, increased capacitance.
--[p194]-- A large $\varepsilon _ { r }$ combined with a thin insulator layer results in the majority of the electric potential being over the air gap. This results in a higher electrostatic pressure. A large ε<sub>r</sub> is not always desirable, since an increasing ε<sub>r</sub> results in a larger dependence on air gap height. The graph below illustrates the dependence of clamping pressure on supply voltage, insulator thickness, dielectric constant and air gap height.
--[p194]-- K.3.2.3 Relative permittivity (dielectric constant)
An important parameter in designing an electrostatic clamp is the permittivity of the insulator layer. The absolute permittivity is a measure of how an electric field is affected by a dielectric medium. This is determined by the ability of the material to polarize as a response to the electric field over this material.
--[p194]-- In the contrary to formula for the electrostatic pressure obtained for the standard situation of just an air gap between the electrodes, Fig. 7‐2 does not show a vertical asymptote at zero air gap thickness. This is due to the presence of the insulator thickness term in the denominator of the formula above. The same term also causes the absence of a logarithmic relation between pressure and air gap, as is illustrated in Fig. 9‐102.
--[p194]-- The relative permittivity, or dielectric constant, is the ratio between the permittivity of a certain matter and the permittivity of free space (absolute permittivity of vacuum).
In the application of a capacitor, the relative permittivity is proportional to the capacitance. Therefore, an increased (relative) permittivity leads to a higher capacitance.
$$
C = \varepsilon _ { 0 } \varepsilon _ { r } { \frac { A } { d } }
$$
--[p194 [表]]-- A table of the relative permittivity of different materials is presented below. More typical values of dielectric constants can be found in [50].
Table 9‐23 Dielectric constant (relative permittivity) of some materials [50]
Material | Relative permittivity
Vacuum | 1 (by definition)
All gases | 1.00xx
Polyimide | 3.4 - 3.5
Water | 34 - 78
Silicon | 11
Rubber | 2- 4
$\ S i O _ { 2 }$ | 4.5

 The current wafer handling system can be used and a second one is needed for the target wafers
 Current location of electronics, pumps and cooling can be kept
o It is however preferred to isolate the system from pump and housing fan vibrations since these have a considerable contribution in the error tree

 Base frame only needs small adjustments and extensions since gantry system is replaced by smaller wafer stage
 The flip units are already fast enough to supply 5 dies/sec. These units are however no longer required in the new design.

===== [p199-199] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.5 Capacitive clamp vs. JR clamp in PnP scope (5块) =====
--[p199]-- K.5 Capacitive clamp vs. JR clamp in PnP scope
There are two limitations of the JR clamp that avoid it from being a useful clamping mechanism for fast PnP with cycle times <200 [ms]. First, the RC time of the JR clamp is much larger than the allowed time frame in which the clamping force should be obtained. The capacitive clamp has much shorter clamping (and declamping) time and is therefore preferred.
The second reason is related to the operating principle.
During clamping with the JR clamp, small currents will flow through the resistors. Below an estimation of the current.
$$
--[p199]-- Because a small leakage current is always present at some level, a solution could be to charge the clamp periodically, dependent on the leakage current.
Because of the smaller cycling time, the lack of charges at the interface and the lower power consumption, the capacitive clamp is preferred over the JR clamp.
--[p199]-- This in turn leads to the risk of creating of creating enough potential over the capacitor plates the breakdown voltage of the dielectric is exceeded.
There is also another advantage of the capacitive clamp. Since it does not require a current flow, the voltage source of the clamp can be disconnected after charging under the condition that no charge can flow between the electrodes.
--[p199]-- \begin{array} { l l l } { { A _ { d i e } = 2 5 \Big [ m m ^ { 2 } \Big ] \to 2 5 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 6 } \Big [ m ^ { 2 } \Big ] } } & { { \qquad } } & { { R = \rho _ { i n s } \frac { t _ { i n s } } { A _ { d i e } } = 4 \cdot 1 0 ^ { 1 2 } \big [ \Omega \big ] } } \\ { { \rho _ { i n s } = 1 0 ^ { 1 2 } \big [ \Omega m \big ] } } & { { \qquad } } & { { I = \frac { U } { R } = 1 0 ^ { - 1 1 } \big [ A \big ] } } \\ { { t _ { i n s } = 1 0 0 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 6 } \big [ m \big ] } } & { { \qquad } } & { { P = R \cdot I ^ { 2 } = 4 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 1 0 } \big [ W \big ] } } \end{array}
$$
Although this is a small current, it will flow continuously. This brings with it the chance of charging the capacitors on the ICs.
--[p199]-- Since JR effect are also present in capacitive clamps, the resistance of the insulator should be chosen in a way that the JR time constant is much larger than the time the die will spend on the clamp. Relating to the earlier example of JR cycle time, the volume resistivity should be larger than ${ 1 0 } ^ { 1 4 }$ [Ω∙cm] to obtain a RC time of several days.

===== [p54-54] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > Chapter 5 Concept outline (2块) =====
--[p54]-- First the general workings of the concept are summarized, after which the alignment principle is discussed in more detail. This is followed by the considerations about the flexible carrier system. The benefits of the flexible carrier system in terms of possible parallel processes are also discussed. Next a summary is given of required critical components and a CAD drawing is shown for illustrative purposes.

The flexible carrier concept was in the previous chapter proposed as the new method of transporting dies from the pick to the place location for 3D‐IC single die wafer‐2‐wafer assembly. The aim of this chapter is to give an overview of the possibilities and limitations of this concept besides the increase in throughput. Of‐the‐shelve components are suggested for some critical functions, parts of the original Datacon machine are also reused.

===== [p102-102] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > B.6.3 Clean room (2块) =====
--[p102]-- B.6.3 Clean room
It is possible that the PnP machine will be placed in a clean room environment. This would be beneficial for the contamination control inside the PnP machine. However, it cannot be assumed that the PnP machine will in fact be placed in a clean room environment since this largely depends on the requirements for the process steps before and after PnP.
--[p102]-- Because of the small dimensions of the bumps on the dies and their susceptibility to contamination it is possible that a class 100.000 clean room environment is required for the contamination control in the PnP machine. Particles larger than the bump height can cause device failure.

===== [p212-212] Appendix L Experimental investigation of electrostatic tape > L.4.1 Characterization of clamp (1块) =====
--[p212]-- L.4.1 Characterization of clamp
It is preferred to do a contactless surface qualification of both the electrodes and the clamps. This allows calculation of the air gap, which in turn allows calculation of the expected clamping force. The preferred method is a surface measurement with the Wyko interferometer.
If this is not possible due to the poor reflective properties of the parts, the capacity of the clamp should be measured. This way the average air gap can also be calculated.
To qualify the surface of the clamp independent of the electrodes a flat electrode (better than 500 nm) should be used.

--[p168]-- J.1.1 Dimensions and pitch of dies
The components to be transported with the flexible carrier vary greatly in dimensions. The surface area of the die ranges from 0.25 [mm<sup>2</sup>] to 250 [mm<sup>2</sup>] (Table 1‐1). It is therefore estimated that the typical dimensions of the dies ranges from 0.5 [mm] to 25 [mm]. The estimated die pitch can be related to these values.

===== [p74-75] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.1.2.1 Die without TSVs, bulk Si side (4块) =====
--[p74]-- 7.1.2.1 Die without TSVs, bulk Si side
The electrostatic wafer clamps in existence today, hold the wafer on the bulk silicon side while the other side goes through the different front‐end process steps. Since the clamping of thick wafers is possible, with clamps operating at several thousand volts, the clamping of bare dies on the bulk Si side should be possible as well. The risk of damage is even more reduced if lower voltages are used.
--[p75]-- Between these layers there is an insulating layer that prevents the electric field from entering the IC, thereby avoiding the risk of applying a too high voltage over the dielectric in transistors and capacitors. Despite the absence of the epilayer in simper dies, it is still very likely that the electric field does not enter the process layer because of the relatively thick bulk silicon layer (see Fig. 7‐7).
--[p75]-- The die basically consists three sections, of which the middle one (epilayer) is optional. This epilayer is only present in dies requiring advanced IC‐processes, for less complicated IC’s this relatively expensive layer is omitted.
On top of the die the process layer is located. This layer contains the actual IC, where the capacitors, transistors and other components are sited. The bottom of the die consists of bulk silicon, where the electric field due to the electrostatic clamp is situated.
--[p74]-- With the industry advancing to thin dies, it becomes increasingly important to understand the effect of a static electric field on the die itself. To support the explanation, a simplified cross section of a die above a bipolar clamp is shown in (Fig. 7‐7).
<sup>12</sup> A semiconductor manufacturer that makes chips for third parties. It may be a large chip maker that sells its excess manufacturing capacity or one that makes chips exclusively for other companies.

===== [p87-87] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 8.2 Recommendations and opportunities (1块) =====
--[p87]-- 8.2 Recommendations and opportunities
With the results obtained in this thesis an actual design can be made for a pick and place tool meeting the requirements of placing 5 dies per second at a placement accuracy of 1 micrometer. Further improvements of the current design can be obtained if further research is carried out. The following recommendations and opportunities were identified.

===== [p124-125] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.4.2.7 Placement process (3块) =====
--[p125]-- The magnitude of the error is defined by the non parallelism, die size and the variation of vertical stroke (die thickness, substrate height variations).
--[p125]-- The error due to breaking loose from the nozzle is caused by friction between the die and substrate at the initial contact position. If this friction is higher than the force keeping the die attached to the nozzle, the die breaks loose. Therefore the position of the die on the substrate cannot be controlled. If the die slips over the substrate the position is also undefined since the bending of the nozzle is unknown. Slipping of the die over the substrate also poses the risk of damage.
--[p124]-- D.4.2.7 Placement process
The placement processed was analyzed in [40]. Below, the problem statement is summarized.
Due to non parallelism of chip relative to substrate two effects that can cause XYRz errors can occur:
 Die breaks loose from nozzle
 Die slips over substrate
Both result in an undefined position of the die on the substrate, with a possible placement error as the result.

===== [p27-27] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > 1.5 Summary and outline (6块) =====
--[p27]-- During this project many different subjects had to be investigated. In order to keep this report well readable, much of the more in depth work is presented in the extensive appendix of this thesis. The main text of this report presents the red line connecting all topics, with in depth information where needed.
--[p27]-- 1.5 Summary and outline
3D integration of chips is being done for some years now with limited market share, using for example wire bonds or solder balls for the electrical connection between the different chips. 3D integration of bare dies with Through Silicon Vias (TSVs) extends this concept and is expected to increase the performance of a chip in terms of speed, power consumption and functionality. These TSVs provide the electrical connection between the dies and have small dimensions.
--[p27]-- Therefore a solution has to be found that combines the required accuracy and throughput at limited machine cost.
This fist chapter gave an impression of the world of 3D‐IC and its drivers, thereby introducing the core topic of this master thesis, namely the conceptual design of a new pick and place machine. The second chapter gives an summary of the identified system requirements for die‐to‐wafer stacking.
--[p27]-- For the connection between dies to be successful, the TSVs of both dies need to be aligned. For the concept of 3D integration of dies to be economically viable, the stacking process of the dies needs to be very fast compared to current state‐of‐the‐art equipment [8]. A machine with the required combination of high accuracy and high throughput for die‐to‐wafer stacking does not yet exist today. When compared to other processes, the pick and place process has the largest contribution to cost.
--[p27]-- For one critical system of the newly developed concept no solution exists today. The design of this system (the new die transport system), is analyzed in chapter six and again concepts are developed. Chapter seven discusses the further analysis of this system. Results of the experiments carried out to verify the potential of the proposed system are finally discussed in this chapter as well. The conclusions and recommendations are stated in the final eight chapter of this MSc. Thesis report.
--[p27]-- Next, an overview is given of the pick and place market and a current state‐of‐the‐art pick and place machine is analyzed, explaining its limitations. Following this analysis, the fourth chapter discusses the developed preliminary concepts and a trade‐off of these concepts results in the chosen concept. An overview of the developed concept is given in chapter five. Commercially available solutions are proposed for several key components.

As the results in Fig.

===== [p28-28] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > Chapter 2 Requirements (1块) =====
--[p28]-- Chapter 2 Requirements
Currently no effective solution exists for the assembly of 3D‐ICs. This is primarily due to the lack of accuracy and throughput of present equipment. This chapter discusses the identified requirements for a pick and place (PnP) machine for die‐to‐wafer stacking in a 3D‐IC application.

===== [p133-134] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > Pick and place cycle (4块) =====
--[p133]-- • v = 6 m/s to stay jerk limited
• In that case, the total cycle time will be: 0.34 s
• Methods to further decrease cycle time:
• Eliminate/parallelize motion steps: parallelize small z-motions large xymotions: gain is 0.1 s: Total cycle time id 0.24 s.
•Decrease travel distance.
•Parallelize multiple robots
--[p134]-- Pick and place cycle
• General conclusions:
• Start-stop costs a lot of cycle time
• → try to avoid stopping
→ try to make one movement from source to target; don't split up in e.g. XY and Z movements
• For minimized setup time, jerk must be limiting (3rd order profiles)
• For jerk limiting profiles, especially acceleration is increased a lot with respect to state-of-the-art solutions
--[p133]-- Pick and place cycle
• Almost 0.5 s cycle time, while using high acceleration of 110 m/s²
• Increasing acceleration does not vield lower cycle time, Only increase in jerk AND acceleration can decrease cycle time
• j = 6000 m/s³ (doubled)
• a = 175 m/s²to stay jerk limited
• v = 5.15 m/s to stay jerk limitec
• In that case, the total cycle time will be: 0.4 s. So doubling jerk will decrease cycle time by only 20%.
• Example 2: Extreme value of
• j = 10000 m/s3
• a = 250 m/s² to stay jerk limited
--[p134]-- • Increasing jerk over \~10000 m/s³ is (relatively) not very effective for reducing cycle times, when travelling \~300 mm. Parallelization will be much more effective in this case.
• 5 dies/s with one robot is not realistic.

===== [p28-28] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > 2.1 Component to be assembled (4块) =====
--[p28]-- The existing TNO process flow for 3D integration of dies states a 300 [mm] wafer as source and as target for the pick and place machine. During pick and place, the dies should not be contaminated on the side that is attached to the stack since this could result in defects. It is also preferred that the other side is not contaminated either.
--[p28]-- The Datacon 8800 FC Quantum (Fig. 2‐1) is the base line for the developed concept. Fig. 2‐2 shows a typical 5x5 [mm] die. The dies to be assembled with the new machine have less than 10% thickness of the illustrated ones.
--[p28]-- Although not a requirement, but a target is nice to have, it is preferred to have the possibility to implement cleaning and inspection provisions before die placement (in between pick and place). The newly developed concept will be based on an existing pick and place machine in the way that parts used in the existing machine (e.g. wafer loader) can be used for the current concept. It is also preferred to have a concept that fits within the current machine volume.
--[p28]-- 2.1 Component to be assembled
The components to be assembled are bare front‐end processed silicon dies with small (<5  μm) conductive bumps or patches on the TSVs. The location of these bumps can be on either or even on both sides of the die (IC side or bare silicon side). These bumps will most likely be either aluminum or copper, but the PnP process should not rule out one of them.

===== [p174-174] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.2.3.2 Electrostatic adhesion (1块) =====
--[p174]-- J.2.3.2 Electrostatic adhesion
Electrostatic adhesion is based on the physical principle that an electric field attracts electric charge. The formula describing this phenomenon is called “Coulomb’s Law”. The formula below shows the simplified form.
$$
\overrightarrow { F _ { A B } } = q _ { A } \cdot \overrightarrow { E _ { B } }
$$
In this formula, E is the electric field, and q is the charge which is attracted by the electric field.

In future research surface measurements should be performed to assess the effect of the damage on the surface quality and air gap.

===== [p198-198] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.4.3 Clamping and declamping times (4块) =====
--[p198]-- K.4.3 Clamping and declamping times
The clamping and declamping time for the JR effect is determined by the resistance of the dielectric material and the air gap capacity. For the circuit shown in Fig. 9‐107, the RC time for the JR clamping is described by:
$$
\tau _ { _ { R C } } = R _ { _ { i n s } } \cdot C _ { _ { a i r } }
$$
This is understand best by looking at the situation where the clamp is discharged by short circuiting the clamp.
--[p198]-- In this situation, the electrons stored in the air gap capacitance travel through both resistances. The resistance of the air layer is generally larger than the insulator resistance. Therefore most charge will mostly flow through the insulator. A calculation of a typical time constant is stated below.
$$
--[p198]-- This is because when charging the clamp, the initial charge is build up over the capacitors in a very short timeframe. This is then followed by charging of the JR clamp. So for the JR clamp, first a very fast but low pressure is build up because of the charging both capacitors, followed by further charging of the air gap capacitor.
--[p198]-- \tau _ { \scriptscriptstyle { R C } } = R _ { i n s } \cdot C _ { a i r } = \rho \frac { d _ { i n s } } { A } \cdot \varepsilon \frac { A } { d _ { a i r } } = \rho \cdot \varepsilon = 1 0 ^ { 1 2 } \cdot 8 , 8 5 4 \cdot 1 0 ^ { - 1 2 } \cdot \frac { 1 0 0 } { 1 0 } \approx 1 0 0 \big [ s \big ]
$$
In reality, two different time constants will be observed, one on JR time and one related to the purely capacitive clamp.

===== [p170-170] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.1.3.1 Bends (1块) =====
--[p170]-- J.1.3.1 Bends
For routing the flexible carrier over bends, the carrier should be flexible over its full length or at least have some elements in it that allow deformation of the carrier. So, it is allowed to have a carrier that behaves more like a bicycle chain. The minimal radius the carrier should be able to handle is set at 100 [mm].

===== [p199-200] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.6.1 Paschen curve (5块) =====
--[p199]-- Paschen’s law states the breakdown voltage as function of pressure, distance and two gas dependent constants
$$
V = { \frac { a \left( p \cdot d \right) } { \ln { \left( p \cdot d \right) } + b } }
$$
Where V is the breakdown voltage in volts, p is the pressure in atmospheres (One atmosphere is equal to 101325 [Pa]) and d is the gap distance. The constants a and b for air at atmospheric pressure are, $a = 4 3 , 6 { \cdot } 1 0 ^ { 6 }$ [V/(atm∙m)] and $b = 1 2 , 8$ .
--[p199]-- The Paschen curve for air at standard atmospheric pressure and changing gap distance is illustrated in Fig. 9‐108.
The minimal breakdown voltage of 327 [V] is found for an air gap of about 7.5 [μm]. The corresponding electrical field is shown in Fig. 9‐109 since this is directly related to the clamping pressure (Fig. 9‐110).
--[p200]-- Gas | Air | $\mathbf { C O } _ { 2 }$ | He | Nitrogen | Oxygen
Breakdown voltage [V] | 330 | 240 | 450 | 420 | 150
Paschen curve point [μm·bar] | 7.2 | 7.5 | 52 | 8.5 | 9.2
--[p199]-- K.6.1 Paschen curve
This section discusses the behavior of the capacitive clamp in an air environment and its limitations due to the properties of the air gap. Paschen’s Law describes the breakdown voltage of parallel plates in a gas as a function of gap distance and pressure. The voltage necessary to arc across the gap decreased with decreasing pressure up to a certain point. It then increased again.
--[p200 [表]]-- The shown Paschen curve is only shown up to a distance of 4 [μm] because Paschen’s law fails to predict the the breakdown voltage accurately for small air gaps and even predicts an infinite arc voltage at ${ \mathsf e } ^ { 1 }$ [μm] (about 2.7 [μm]). The trend for these small air gaps is visible though. For illustrative purposes, the minimal breakdown voltage and corresponding Paschen curve point are shown in Table 9‐24 for several gases.
Table 9‐24 Paschen data for several gases

===== [p178-178] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.2.3.3.3 Continuous supply channels in carrier (4块) =====
--[p178]-- This in turn requires some kind of flexible seal in the carrier that opens to the supply tubing and seals around it. This is similar to the electrical connections in some old alternating current generators (dragging contacts).
Second, the vacuum should be maintained inside the carrier at a low enough pressure, despite of nozzles that are not covered with dies, bends in the carrier (additional resistance, Fig.
--[p178]-- 9‐82) and the limited diameter of the internal channels (limited by carrier dimensions). Also, if small obstruction occurs which blocks the channel, the vacuum at the dies could be lost (Fig. 9‐83). And therefore the dies could be dropped from the carrier. These obstructions could occur due to contamination in the channels or due to a deformation of the carrier which reduces the cross‐sectional area of the carrier.
--[p178]-- J.2.3.3.3 Continuous supply channels in carrier
Pressurized channels inside the carrier can be used to bring the vacuum to the dies. The dies are held by individual nozzles supplied by the main channel. The benefit of this concept is that the airflow can be partially sealed off to one die to allow it to be removed from the carrier at reduced holding pressure. However this concept may seem simple, it is actually quite complex. The following concerns were identified for this concept.
--[p178]-- First, it is difficult to continuously supply a moving carrier with a vacuum since the vacuum pump should be connected with tubes to the moving carrier. The supply tubing should then move with the carrier. This is like keeping your bicycle pump attached to the tire while cycling. An alternative is to use a fixed location in space to supply the vacuum to the carrier. When the carrier reaches the supply tube, it opens up, when it passes the tube it closes again.

===== [p115-115] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.3.2 Timing diagram (3块) =====
--[p115]-- Timings of processes that were not found in the available literature were assessed by interpretations of videos of the machine in operation and during auto calibration. The timing diagram is included in Table 9‐8. This diagram was made with Microsoft Project. The smallest measure of time in this program is minutes. For the timing diagram minutes represent milliseconds.
--[p115]-- D.3.2 Timing diagram
The timing diagram states the time it takes to perform the different steps taken by the machine during the pick and place cycle for a single gantry. The timing diagram gives insight in the performance limiting factor from a throughput perspective. While interpreting the diagram one should keep in mind that the timing values are estimates and not measured values. Travel times were calculated using a 3<sup>rd</sup> order setpoint motion profile.
--[p115]-- Also, the diagram is for a single gantry, some processes are however shared between both gantries e.g. the wafer stage and the placement substrate. From evaluating the timing diagram one can conclude that the gantry is always moving and therefore it is the limiting process in terms of throughput.
The timing diagram has been created based on standard documentation and the adjuster guide [35].

===== [p24-25] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > 1.3.3 Cost of ownership (3块) =====
--[p24]-- 1.3.3 Cost of ownership
As is visible in the road map, not only functionality is added, cost per wafer is reduced as well. Stacking one layer of dies on a 300 [mm] wafer currently costs about \$450. The aim is to reduce this to \$150 in 2016. To accomplish this, a cost of ownership (CoO) analysis was performed based on state‐of‐art equipment. From this analysis, critical (expensive) process steps were determined and improvements investigated.
--[p25]-- As is visible from the CoO analysis, the actual stacking process with current pick and place equipment is relatively expensive and needs to be reduced to fit the road map. This is the main reason for the development of new pick and place equipment, the main topic of the current master thesis.
--[p24]-- All these improvements were based on the existing knowledge base of the partners involved. Once the proposed developments have been fully implemented, it is expected that the cost for vertical integration is reduced to \$150 per wafer layer. The TSV diameter is reduced as well (from 20 [μm] to 5 [μm]) to allow more connections per surface area and reduce loss of space for the actual IC on the die.

--[p213]-- L.5 Measurement results
Of both designed clamps only the one with the Kapton HN film as insulator proved to work, therefore the results presented here only apply to this clamp. The failing of the SiO<sub>2</sub> clamp is discussed in section L.6.

===== [p205-205] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > K.8.3 Reduced current (1块) =====
--[p205]-- K.8.3 Reduced current
By replacing the step input for a bit smoother profile, the current through the die can be reduced. This is illustrated in Fig. 9‐107 where a cycloid profile was used between 10 [ms] and 10.01 [ms], increasing the clamping time to 10 [μs]. The maximum current through the die was reduced from 2 [mA] to 0.4 [mA].

===== [p153-153] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > G.2.11 Bond process flexibility (2块) =====
--[p153]-- This criteria rates how well it is possible to change the bond head after the final design. Important parameters here are, number of bond heads in the system and available space around the bond head.
--[p153]-- G.2.11 Bond process flexibility
3D stacking is a new process. Therefore it is possible that the bonding method for bonding one die to another will change over time. Currently it is assumed a temporary bond is made by the PnP machine. Changing the bonding method could mean that the bond head itself has to change. It is preferred to minimize the changes to the machine when the bond process changes. It is even more preferred to have a machine that can change bond head easily.

===== [p42-42] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > Chapter 4 Concept development (2块) =====
--[p42]-- Chapter 4 Concept development
Resulting from the analysis in Chapter 3, it is clear that the cycle time, and therefore throughput, is limited by the way the dies are supplied by the gantry system. Increasing gantry speeds will not suffice in the search for increased throughput. This results in the conclusion that a new concept is required for the component supply system of a die to wafer pick and place machine.
--[p42]-- Besides increasing gantry operating speeds, the throughput can be increased by avoiding starting and stopping, as well as by introducing more parallelism and shortening the travel distance.

 And other items

--[p137]-- F.2 Component supply concepts
Before the die can be placed, it needs to be transported from the source to the placement location, with possible intermediate stops for die preparation or measurement. The source is a 300 [mm] wafer and the target as well. Therefore, on average, the distance to be covered is in the order of 300 [mm]. Several concepts (for die to wafer assembly) are discussed here. These concepts take into account the pickup time, flip time and transport time.

===== [p86-87] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 8.1.3 Experimental verification (3块) =====
--[p86]-- 8.1.3 Experimental verification
A review of clamping mechanisms resulted in two likely candidates for temporarily attaching a die to a flexible carrier. The Diels‐Alder adhesive was not further investigated since the adhesive is still under development.
A bipolar, low voltage, capacitive electrostatic clamp with two D‐shaped electrodes was build and tested.
--[p87]-- The measured performance of the clamp was in correspondence with the mathematical model and provided enough clamping force to withstand the estimated disturbance forces. Therefore, the design was successful. It was also found that it is important to use a clamp without residual particles or charge on it, since this effects the clamping force. The JR effect should be avoided to achieve fast clamping and declamping times.
--[p87]-- The risk of damaging the ICs on the dies is unlikely if the air gap voltage is kept below the breakdown voltage of the transistors and capacitors on the dies. The operating voltage of current transistors is 1.8 – 5 [V], the breakdown voltage is higher. The air gap voltage for sufficient clamping was found to be in the order of 5 – 20 Volt. The minimal oxide‐layer thickness is 0.7 [nm], with a corresponding estimated breakdown voltage of 0.5 – 0.7 [V].
With reducing oxide thickness it therefore becomes increasingly important to understand the failure mechanisms of ICs due to ESD.

===== [p153-153] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > G.2.4 Possibility for parallel measurement (1块) =====
--[p153]-- G.2.4 Possibility for parallel measurement
For the calculation of the estimated UPH the measurement steps were not included. If the measurement can be performed during another step listed in the Gantt chart, the throughput is not negatively affected by the measurement step.

===== [p223-223] Appendix L Experimental investigation of electrostatic tape > L.8.2.3 Residual charges (2块) =====
--[p223]-- L.8.2.3 Residual charges
If for some reason, e.g. attracting charges from the air, additional charge is created at the interface of the insulator and sample electrode, the electric field is altered. This results in a larger clamping force than can be obtained the capacitive clamp alone. The existence of these charges was identified by short circuiting the electrodes of the clamp. The remaining charge at the interface caused the clamping force.
--[p223]-- The residual charge was removed easily by whipping the surfaces with alcohol.

===== [p75-75] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 7.1.2.3 Process layer (1块) =====
--[p75]-- 7.1.2.3 Process layer
During transport from the pick to the place location it is possible that the die has to be grasped on the process layer side as well. This makes it very likely that an electric field will be applied over the IC.

===== [p120-120] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.4.2.1 Marker (2块) =====
--[p120]-- D.4.2.1 Marker
In many procedures markers are used to determine the position and angle of the gantry, die, substrate, etc. The measurement can only be as good as its reference, therefore the limited accuracy of markers is an error source. Different markers that are used are for example:
 10 mm pitch glass plate for base calibration (accuracy estimated at 100 [nm])
 G0 markers for setting the global co‐ordinate system (accuracy estimated at 100 [nm])
--[p120]--  FOCT markers (thermal compensation) (accuracy estimated at 100 [nm])
 Markers on dies (accuracy estimated at 500 [nm])
Markers on substrate (accuracy estimated at 700 [nm])
 Solder ball array on die

===== [p116-116] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > D.4.1.2.1 Substrate camera calibration / focus length (1块) =====
--[p116]-- D.4.1.2.1 Substrate camera calibration / focus length
The focus length and camera center is determined with an auto focus function and an integrated touchdown sensor. A circle is measured in 3 edges of the video screen and also in the center.
Measured values: XY camera scaling, camera rotation, camera center
Because the gantry axis are moving during this routine, vibration due to roller guides and reaction forces are important as well as external vibrations.
The G0 marker is measured with the SC, therefore their imperfections are to be taken into account.

===== [p145-145] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > F.7 Linear guide (2块) =====
--[p145]-- F.7 Linear guide
Another way of separating the supply system from the placement system is by using some sort of linear transport system between the pick position and the place position, creating a buffer of dies near the place location. The dies have to be flipped after the slide to avoid contamination of, or damage to the underside of the die (which will be attached to previous die in stack).
--[p145]-- The source wafer, placed on a wafer stage, moves under a predefined position where the die will be picked of the wafer and placed on a linear guide. At the end of the linear guide, near the placement location, a flip unit flips the die and it is placed by a single nozzle gantry system. The xy alignment can be located at the placement nozzle or at the target location.
$$
t _ { t o t } = 2 \cdot t _ { p r e s } + t _ { p i c k }
$$

Before the actual working can be discusses assumptions have to be made about what enters the machine and what exits it. Since the Bluebird process flow is still partially under development, these assumptions also make the workings easier to explain.

===== [p168-169] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.1.2.1 Travel time and motion profile parameters (5块) =====
--[p169 [表]]-- For the 5 [mm] dies, the travel time of 50 [ms] should be acceptable. As seen in Fig. 9‐71, the profiles are jerk limited for short distances and also acceleration limited for larger distances. When increasing the allowed parameters the travel times were decreased to 16, 31 and 59 [ms] (Fig. 9‐72). The overview of both is stated in Table 9‐20.
Table 9‐20 Overview of travel times for two motion profile settings
--[p168]-- This was reached with the following motion profile parameters.
$\mathtt { v } _ { \mathtt { m a x } } = 2 [ \mathsf { m } / \mathsf { s } ]$
$\mathsf { a } _ { \mathsf { m a x } } = 2 5 [ \mathsf { m } / \mathsf { s } ^ { 2 } ]$
$$
\begin{array} { r l } { \bullet } & { { } \mathrm { j } = 2 0 0 0 [ { \sf m } / { \sf s } ^ { 3 } ] } \end{array}
$$
These values are the operating values of the gantry system currently used in the Datacon machine.
--[p168]-- J.1.2.1 Travel time and motion profile parameters
For a throughput of 5 dies/sec, the total time to place a single die is 200 [ms]. The time to move the flexible carrier for presenting the next die is part of this time. Therefore the time to present the next die should be significantly shorter than 200 [ms]. The earlier analysis of the timing diagram for the flexible carrier concept (Appendix F.8) proposed a travel time of about 50 [ms].

===== [p29-29] Department of Precision and Microsystems Engineering > 2.2 Accuracy (2块) =====
--[p29]-- 2.2 Accuracy
The accuracy requirement for 3D integration using TSVs results from the fact that the TSVs of separate dies need to be in proper contact to conduct electricity. Smaller dies with more TSVs result in smaller TSVs. It is expected that in a few years the maximum allowable relative position error between any set of TSVs is about 1 [μm] 3σ. This results from the TSV overlap requirement. The largest dimension of a single die is expected to be 25 [mm].
--[p29]-- Therefore, the angular error should be in the order of $5 { \cdot } 1 0 ^ { - 3 }$ degrees or smaller (app. B.1). Although not a requirement, it is preferred to develop a concept that uses the alignment system newly developed by TNO. This reduces the overall design cost and risk of the system. A short overview of the alignment system is presented in app. H.1. Since this product is confidential, no detailed information can be presented.

--[p209 [表]]-- ACCURACY
± 0.1% of full-scale
Calibration temperature: 20°C ± 2°C
Operating temperature: 10°C - 35°℃
Temperature shift at zero load: ± 0.01% of full-scale/°C

===== [p58-58] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 5.2.3 Probing / testing (2块) =====
--[p58]-- 5.2.3 Probing / testing
The inspection step can also be expanded to check the electric circuits on the die. Usually, the good dies on a wafer are identified in a separate machine by a process called probing. Electrical contact is made with the die to test the electric circuits. Non‐contact processes also exist. The flexible carrier concept allows for the integration of the probing step between pick and place. This way, the dies are tested as close as possible to the actual assembly.
--[p58]-- This reduces the risk of damaging the dies after probing and therefore the change of assembling a faulty die. Integrating the probing between pick and place also means one less machine is required, potentially reducing the cost of the total packaging line.

===== [p60-60] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 5.4.1.1 Alignment system (1块) =====
--[p60]-- 5.4.1.1 Alignment system
The alignment system of choice is the co‐location vision system, as discussed in the previous section.

===== [p54-54] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 5.1.1 Input and output (4块) =====
--[p54]-- A side note here is that there is a possibility that particles remain in the dicing streets, this is one of the reasons why cleaning between pick and place is preferred. The next assumption is that the wafer on which the stacks need to be made (target wafer) is loaded and flux or adhesive was applied to the previous layer of the stacks (either inside this PnP machine or with a separate machine). This also implies that the stacking of dies is done one layer at the time.
--[p54]-- 5.1.1 Input and output
It is assumed that a full 300 [mm] wafer with 5x5 [mm] dies enters the PnP machine. At this stage the wafer has TSVs in it and the wafer thickness has been reduced. The reduction in thickness (grinding) is the reason why the bulk silicon side is the exposed side of the wafer, since this side was processed in a previous machine. It is also assumed that the exposed side of the wafer has been cleaned so no particles remain here, and that the wafer has been diced.
--[p54]-- To avoid unnecessary confusion, it is assumed in this chapter that only 5x5 [mm] dies are stacked of different technologies. Although different die sizes in a single stack are not discussed, this does belong to the possibilities of the current concept.
--[p54]-- The possibility of creating full stacks in one run is not discussed here, but the current concept does make this possible if multiple parallel nozzles are used.
The output of the PnP machine is the target wafer, with one more layer on it than when it entered the PnP machine. Currently it is assumed that the final (electrical) bond is created outside the PnP machine and that the PnP machine only makes a temporary connection between dies with adhesive (see requirements in Chapter 2).

===== [p68-68] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 6.4 Choice of mechanism (3块) =====
--[p68]-- This is especially true if a chemical adhesive is engineered such that it has switchable adhesive properties, is reusable and leaves no harmful contamination. Since such an adhesive does not exist today, the electrostatic clamp concept was chosen for further study. When designing a die transport system based on electrostatic clamps, special effort should be put into finding a solution that is only mildly susceptible to contamination and to die and flexible carrier surface quality.
--[p68]-- 6.4 Choice of mechanism
When comparing the advantages and disadvantages of the electrostatic clamp and the vacuum concept, it is obvious that the electrostatic concept shows more potential due to the limitations of the vacuum concept in supplying and maintaining the holding force. Comparing the electrostatic clamp and the chemical adhesion concept, both have good prospects.
--[p68]-- A low cost electrostatic clamp should be aimed for and the risk of damaging the electrical circuits on the die has to be further investigated as well in an early stage of the carrier design process.
Experimental  investigation  of  critical system (flexible carrier)

===== [p143-143] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > F.5.2 Nozzles axial (2块) =====
--[p143]-- Advantages and disadvantages same as above, with the added disadvantage of minimal turret size and more difficult access to nozzles during nozzle replacement. Added advantage is that both pickup and placement can be performed in the same orientation in the horizontal plane (same side up).
--[p143]-- F.5.2 Nozzles axial
Similar to the previous process, the dies are picked up by a flip unit and presented to a turret. The difference is that the nozzles are positioned axial instead of radial to the turret disc. This implementation however causes a minimal size for the turret since the target wafer needs to be able to ‘slide’ under the placement position of the disc. Therefore, the minimal diameter of the turret is greater than 300 mm.

--[p182]-- J.3.2 Accelerations
According to the $2 ^ { \mathsf { n d } }$ law of Newton, the inertia of the die causes a force proportional to the acceleration of the die. The two accelerations acting on the die are the accelerations due to the motion profile of the displacement and secondly, the gravitational acceleration. ===== [p170-170] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.1.5 Cost (2块) =====
--[p170]-- These components are part of the dicing tape release mechanism and the actual placement system respectively.
The carrier system should have minimal operating cost, since this cost is directly added to the stacking cost. For this reason, machine consumables should be minimized and a reusable carrier is highly preferred.
--[p170]-- J.1.5 Cost
It is aimed for that the total cost of the parts should does not exceed a few percent of the total cost. After all, the flexible carrier system is a low accuracy system so no expensive measurement equipment has to be used. The flexible carrier system cost includes the carrier itself, the guidance systems and any additional equipment supplying the hold force, The tools for placing the die on the carrier or releasing it are not included here.

===== [p170-170] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > J.1.3.3 Accessible area (1块) =====
--[p170]-- J.1.3.3 Accessible area
At the placement location it should be possible to approach either side of the die with a vacuum nozzle. This is no problem for the side not attached to the carrier. For the attached side it is estimated that an area of 10% of the die size should be sufficient to attach such a nozzle.

===== [p51-51] Chapter 3 Analysis current pick and place equipment > 4.4.3 Wafer to wafer (3块) =====
--[p51]-- However, if one achieves a high repeatability die release and place process, the offset error can be compensated for in software and the accurate alignment of the full wafers could be sufficient to obtain the required accuracy specifications. There is however a challenge in finding a suitable (source) wafer stage that provides access (preferably mechanical) to the dicing tape and also provides enough stiffness to avoid the diced wafer from bending.
--[p51]-- 4.4.3 Wafer to wafer
Both wafers are aligned first. Next the die is released from the source wafer and placed on the target wafer.   
This can be done with several mechanisms.
 One can for instance use a nozzle that pushes the die from the dicing tape<sup>9</sup> in a way similar to ejection pins, this nozzle can then also provide the final alignment.
 The die can also be released contactless by creating a repelling force between source wafer and die.
--[p51]-- Such a solution was developed in the laser die transfer project [17].
Measurement of the die position itself is difficult because of limited space, making the die and target inaccessible. This makes it difficult to achieve the required accuracy (1 [μm] 3σ) with this concept.
